The Nervous System

4 The Nervous System

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why must a moving machine keep checking its commands when its learned model stops responding?

A learned model can pause for a fraction of a second while it interprets a scene, but the machine carrying it keeps moving, and a loaded base covers tens of centimeters in that pause. Every delay between a decision and the current it becomes is therefore paid in clearance. A general-purpose computer hides such delays behind queues, retries, and priorities. A moving machine cannot, because a command that arrives late acts on a body that has already moved, and a check that waits on the model it checks protects nothing once that model stalls. Treating timing and communication as implementation details produces machines whose safeguards share a processor, a supply rail, or a memory bus with the model they guard, and so fail together with it. A machine that acts on learned decisions therefore needs a timed path between proposal and actuator whose delays are bounded, whose checks never wait on the proposer, and whose grant of authority to each proposal expires on its own. At the causal boundary, time granted to a proposal becomes motion that software cannot recall.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why the permission path’s independence from the proposer is a requirement to be demonstrated rather than a property of separate processors
  • Derive update rates and loop placement from actuator time constants, the phase lag of sampling and transport delay, and proposal renewal limits
  • Evaluate mailbox exchanges for bounded reads, complete payloads, and proposal freshness when the writer stalls
  • Size a chunk lease between the renewal floor and the pre-brake ceiling, and explain why a host heartbeat cannot renew it
  • Distinguish measured latency percentiles from response-time bounds that include computation, blocking, and interference
  • Compare position, velocity, torque, and impedance commands by what each leaves the permission path to check
  • Evaluate interface timing violations using handoff records that distinguish allocated budgets, supporting evidence, and unresolved bounds
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

4.1 The Multi-Rate Bridge

The running machine joins two computational regimes that run on clocks roughly three orders of magnitude apart. A deep neural network processes camera frames into action proposals over tens to hundreds of milliseconds, with latency that fluctuates from frame to frame. An electric motor drive, by contrast, regulates winding current every few tens of microseconds, in a loop that can never pause because the mass it moves keeps its momentum whether or not a new command arrives. The architectural layer between them, the machine’s Nervous System, routes action proposals downward toward the actuators, streams physical telemetry back up, and arbitrates whether any proposal is permitted to move the hardware. Industrial motion control, avionics, and automotive engineering solved similar coordination problems decades ago for classical feedback controllers whose worst-case execution times were statically bounded before deployment (Kopetz 2011). Learned proposers break those classical assumptions. Their inference latencies have long tails, their software runtimes can stall unexpectedly, and their outputs can fail in ways that cannot be exhaustively enumerated at design time.

This asymmetry sets the chapter’s central design question, which The Cognitive Brain left open with two proposers (a chunk policy and an intent model, either of which can stall, miss its deadline, or misjudge the target). The question is how long the physical plant may keep executing the last valid proposal when an inference step stalls or drops a frame. That duration cannot be an arbitrary software timeout or the proposer’s own schedule; it must follow from the machine’s stopping distance and mechanical limits.

On the sense-plan-act (S·P·A) triad (The Sense–Plan–Act Paradigm), proposals cross from Plan to Act only through the permission path.

On the warehouse mobile manipulator, that gap appears every time the vision-language-action (VLA) policies plan a grasp on a conveyor-belt mug while the brushless motor drives regulate winding current thousands of times per second. If motor regulation ever blocks on deliberative reasoning, or contends for shared memory without a bound on its wait, a heavy inference burst can push the control loop past its hard deadline. Moving mass and electromagnetic coils force one conclusion. The Brain needs time to deliberate, while the Body needs continuous control.

Coupling these domains across a shared blocking execution path forces the fast loop to inherit the tail latency of the slow one. If the motor control loop stalls waiting for inference or drains an outdated FIFO queue of stale setpoints, the resulting actuation commands arrive at a physical body that has already moved past the target state. Unmanaged transport delay (\(\PureTransportDelay\)) also injects phase lag directly into the feedback loop, adding \(\DelayPhaseLag = \AngularFrequency \PureTransportDelay\) radians at angular frequency \(\AngularFrequency\). That lag erodes the loop’s phase margin, the extra lag it can absorb before its corrections start to reinforce the motion instead of damping it (made precise in section 4.3.1). The Nervous System must bound these end-to-end transport delays deterministically while ensuring that high-rate local control remains autonomous whenever high-level deliberation stalls.

Each tier runs at least ten times faster than the one above it, and none may wait on a slower one.

To enforce this isolation, the Nervous System splits work across four tiers, separating what each tier controls alongside how often it runs. Each tier runs decoupled from the tiers above it, so fast safety and stabilization loops never wait on slower deliberative tiers:

  1. Actuator current and velocity regulation: Dedicated motor drives close the inner winding-current and joint-velocity loops locally in hardware. Field-oriented control (FOC)1 aligns the stator voltage vector with the rotor’s magnetic flux in every carrier period (the switching period of the drive’s pulse-width modulation, or PWM). The winding then produces the commanded torque within a few current-loop samples.
  2. Permission and position supervision: The deterministic real-time supervisory controller verifies proposal freshness and kinematic limits, sends admitted position setpoints across the fieldbus, checks tracking error against physical safety envelopes, and commands a fail-safe stopping trajectory if proposal authority expires.
  3. Action-chunk policy: The learned mid-level policy emits a short sequence of future waypoints (an action chunk), giving local controllers a continuous motion segment to track between discrete inference steps.
  4. Intent planning: Slower vision-language or task-planning models read camera frames and language instructions to set high-level goals, steering the action-chunk policy without directly touching drive setpoints.

1 Field-Oriented Control (FOC): The drive rotates the three measured phase currents into a frame that turns with the rotor’s magnetic field, where torque and flux become two slowly varying currents that ordinary proportional-integral loops can hold. The rotation needs a fresh rotor angle in every carrier period, so the loop cannot wait on anything slower than its own sensors. That is why the current loop runs inside the drive, beside the encoder and the current shunts, and why no proposer, however fast, ever sits inside it.

Section 4.3.1 gives each tier its rate, while one rule holds all four together and comes first: a stalled or crashed proposer must never block or delay a lower tier’s checks. Conventional computing architectures break that rule because they rely on shared processors, unified memory buses, and multi-level caches managed by general-purpose operating systems. In such environments, hardware isolation is weak. When a large deep neural network streams gigabytes of model weights from DRAM across a shared memory fabric, its high-throughput direct memory access (DMA) bursts contend directly with real-time telemetry and control packets. Because hardware bus transactions cannot be preempted by software thread priorities, control packets queue up at the memory controller. A control path that shares an execution environment with machine learning runtimes is also exposed to operating-system jitter, because kernel page faults, garbage collection cycles, driver interrupts, and thermal frequency throttling can each stall execution for tens of milliseconds. Physical stability therefore demands an architecture in which local control loops keep updating the actuators on schedule whatever delays the inference path suffers.

The physical numbers show why the fast tiers cannot wait. An electromagnetic motor winding has an electrical time constant (\(\ElectricalTimeConstant = \PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\)) of a few milliseconds, 2.7 ms for the running machine’s direct-drive hub motors (Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping), while the moving links carry substantial kinetic energy (\(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \BodySpeed^2\)). Delaying or dropping an actuation command by a fraction of a second can exhaust the clearance before a new inference cycle finishes. On the warehouse robot the mismatch appears in two mechanical domains:

  • The base (Class 1) cruises at up to its 1.5 m/s drive limit (illustrative; see the Reader Guide). At this speed, it travels 60 mm during a single 40 ms action-chunk inference, and 503 mm across one 336 ms intent-model inference cycle. Unless an independent supervisory process monitors plant telemetry and commands braking, the chassis travels half a meter open-loop before deliberative planning produces an updated command, nearly half the rack-end clear distance on the machine card.
  • The arm (Class 2) couples motor rotors to joints through high-ratio gearboxes, reflecting rotor inertia back to the joint output as \(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\), the scaling derived in Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching. The drives keep closing their own joint loops when the proposer stalls, but they keep tracking the last admitted command. If that command was set for a grasp on a conveyor mug or a turn of a door latch the scene no longer matches, the reflected inertia and the drives together keep pressing the mechanism into the contact. Contact forces then escalate rapidly. Whether this interaction breaches structural limits depends on the contact compliance, surface friction, and mechanical response times alongside the reflected inertia.

↩︎ The intent model’s inference latency, priced from its compute-bound prefill and its bandwidth-bound weight sweeps, is established in The memory wall.

Software discipline, operating system thread priorities, and runtime exception handlers cannot by themselves keep the plant safe. Safety requires an explicit, physically deterministic computing, memory, and communication fabric. That fabric forms the Nervous System of the five-level machine model (The Machine in Five Levels), hosting the dedicated permission path so the physical plant retains a measured, feasible response whenever higher-level inference pauses or fails. Its primary design requirement is an interface whose temporal bounds, command authority, and fault responses can be verified directly against the machine’s physical safety budget. Enforcement algorithms later turn these requirements into per-tick admission decisions, while the mechanisms of this layer fix the contracts those decisions must satisfy.

Building and pricing that fabric proceeds from silicon isolation to the system handoff record. Section 4.2 shows why the timed path to the actuators must remain independent of unbounded interference and stalls from deliberative inference, stating the two-processor split that meets this requirement as a hypothesis the machine’s silicon must still confirm under load. Section 4.3 derives the rate of each tier from a physical time constant and passes proposals between tiers through a mailbox that never blocks the faster reader. Section 4.4 then answers the chapter’s central question with the chunk lease, the bounded interval during which an admitted proposal may keep driving the actuators, priced in the stopping budget. Section 4.5 bounds the computation and the drive network (the fieldbus) on which that price depends. Because bounded timing still requires exclusive control of the actuators and survival under host faults, section 4.6 and section 4.7 keep the permission path the sole writer of the drives and keep it alive when the proposer fails, while section 4.8 states the execution contract its checks must meet however fast the proposer runs. Finally, the handoff record of section 4.9 locates every limit at one of five interfaces, coupling this layer’s lease to the stopping distance of the Body and the renewal rate of the Brain.

↪ The per-tick admission decisions that must satisfy these contracts are developed in The Per-Tick Gatekeeper.

4.2 The Independence Requirement and the Two-Processor Hypothesis

When an inference model suffers a transient stall or drops a frame, the robot can safely reject the stale proposal and fall back to local control. When a permission check stalls, however, the delay burns directly through the machine’s stopping budget, regardless of whether the processor eventually catches up. The two workloads therefore optimize for different guarantees. A neural chunk policy needs high compute throughput and memory bandwidth to stream motion proposals at its target renewal rate, whereas the permission path needs a strict, provable upper bound on the time required to validate and dispatch each step.

Standard operating-system schedulers decide which ready task executes next, but software priority alone cannot guarantee deterministic timing. Raising a control task’s priority reduces processor contention, yet it cannot prevent microarchitectural delays. A sudden page fault suspends execution while the virtual memory subsystem resolves the missing page. Lock contention on shared kernel primitives leaves a real-time thread waiting behind uncritical background tasks. Memory requests stall at the arbiter while DMA transfers from a GPU or neural accelerator saturate the system interconnect. Long inference latency tails2 therefore threaten closed-loop stability even when average proposal latency remains well within nominal bounds.

2 Inference Latency Distribution: The median and \(\text{p99}\) alone do not fix the exceedance probability at a given deadline, which must be measured or modeled separately. Tail-model assumptions and exceedance estimates require representative workload data and an account of dependence and uncertainty; Extreme Value Theory and Tail Risk develops these conditions.

3 Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET): A static WCET is a bound proved over every path through the binary and every state the processor’s caches and pipeline can be in; analyzers compute it by abstract interpretation of the code against a model of that hardware (Wilhelm et al. 2008). Caches, branch predictors, and shared buses make that model either pessimistic or intractable, so a timed path earns a tight bound by avoiding them. The bounded sources listed in section 4.5.1 therefore favor tightly coupled memory and fixed pipelines, giving up average speed for a bound that analysis can prove.

Wilhelm, Reinhard, Jakob Engblom, Andreas Ermedahl, Niklas Holsti, Stephan Thesing, David Whalley, Guillem Bernat, et al. 2008. “The Worst-Case Execution-Time Problem: Overview of Methods and Survey of Tools.” ACM Transactions on Embedded Computing Systems 7 (3): 36:1–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/1347375.1347389.

Bounding the permission tick against those microarchitectural stalls requires separating the parts of its cycle using the standard terms of real-time scheduling (Buttazzo 2011; Kopetz 2011). The permission tick runs as a periodic task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\) released once every period \(\TaskPeriod_i\), set here to 1 ms. Each release is a single job that must finish before its relative deadline \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i\), measured from the release instant and set here to 400 μs. The deadline is shorter than the period so that each admitted setpoint is ready within the cycle that computed it rather than one tick later, a delay whose cost section 4.3.1 prices. It also leaves the rest of the period free for lower-priority work. A deadline is hard when a single miss can cause a physical hazard, as on the permission tick, where one late check adds one more millisecond of unchecked motion. The total wall-clock time from task release to job completion is its response time \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i\). The part of that response time spent running the task’s own instructions is bounded above by its worst-case execution time (WCET)3 \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\).

Definition 4.1: Worst-case execution time

Worst-case execution time is the longest time a task can take to run to completion on a given processor, over every admissible input and every state its caches, pipeline, and buses can be in, with no other task competing for the processor.

  1. Significance: It is the \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) term of every response bound in this chapter, and every margin above it is sized on it. The permission tick declares 250 μs against an unloaded run of 135 μs. The 115 μs between them is a claim that analysis must justify before the 400 μs deadline, and the stopping budget behind it, can rely on the number.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a mean or a high percentile such as \(\text{p99}\), which describe the executions a test happened to reach, the WCET covers every admissible path and processor state. A declared WCET is a third quantity, a number the design commits to, which evidence must show is no smaller than the true one.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording the largest measured execution time as the WCET. A measured maximum can refute a declared bound but never establish it, because a test campaign reaches only some of the processor states in which the code can run (Wilhelm et al. 2008).

The rest of the response time is spent waiting rather than running. Blocking \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) is the time a task spends waiting for a lower-priority task to release a shared hardware or software resource, whereas interference \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) is the time taken by higher-priority tasks, kernel interrupts, and exception handlers that preempt the job. Because blocking and interference vary from one release to the next, the exact timing of recurring events shifts from cycle to cycle, a variation called jitter. Task releases, sensor samples, and job completions each have their own jitter, so a tick released on schedule can still miss its completion deadline if its internal wait times fluctuate. Later, section 4.5.1 combines \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\), \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\), and \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) into a provable bound on \(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i\).

Placed on one axis, the terms separate an illustrative unloaded observation from the bound the schedule assumes (figure 1). The unloaded run of 135 μs is an illustrative observation, not a reported measurement. The 115 μs above it, up to the declared 250 μs, is a claim that WCET analysis must certify. The slack from there to the 400 μs deadline is all the room left for blocking and interference. Underneath, the base covers 1.5 mm during every tick at its 1.5 m/s drive limit, so each of these microsecond terms is ultimately paid in millimeters.

Blocking introduces an architectural vulnerability that priority schemes alone cannot resolve. In priority inversion, a high-priority task waits for a shared resource held by a low-priority task, while medium-priority tasks that need no shared resource preempt the lock holder and so starve the high-priority task for as long as they run. Only a real-time locking protocol, such as priority inheritance or priority ceiling emulation, can bound that wait. The recurring resets of the 1997 Mars Pathfinder spacecraft are the canonical case of an inversion left unbounded; war story 4.1 in section 4.5.1 traces it once the response-time bound it violated is in hand.

POSIX real-time extensions (such as SCHED_FIFO priority scheduling and memory locking via mlockall()), real-time Linux kernels (PREEMPT_RT), and dual-kernel architectures (such as Xenomai) target specific software sources of delay. Locking physical memory via mlockall() eliminates demand paging inside the timed control loop. Assigning processor affinity confines threads to specific CPU cores to avoid migration latency and cache thrashing. Priority inheritance elevates a low-priority lock holder’s priority whenever a high-priority task requests its mutex. These operating-system mechanisms, however, guarantee determinism only when supported by proper configuration, bounded system calls, and verified timing evidence.

↪ The scheduling, interrupt, and memory mechanisms whose formal contribution enters a response bound are developed in Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability.

Shared hardware introduces delays that operating-system software priorities cannot control. A control task cannot read an optical encoder buffer while a high-bandwidth peripheral monopolizes a shared PCI Express (PCIe) link or system memory bus. Any physical infrastructure that two processing domains share, whether system memory, power regulation, a thermal path, or a reference oscillator, can delay or silence the permission path without a single software fault. Cataloging those physical couplings and gathering the evidence required to verify their isolation belongs to placement (Two Paths and the Resources They Share). The requirement that analysis tests can be stated now. Under any workload the proposer can generate, the permission path must meet its response bound and keep its authority to refuse an unsafe motion. This chapter calls that requirement independence.

The running machine meets the independence requirement by putting the two paths on two processors, a hypothesis it must still test (figure 2). An application processor runs perception, the 2 Hz intent model, and the 20 Hz chunk policy. A separate safety microcontroller unit (MCU) runs the permission path and alone drives the motors. Between them runs a board link, a serial peripheral interface (SPI) or Controller Area Network with Flexible Data-rate (CAN FD) bus carrying framed packets with cyclic redundancy checks (CRCs) and sequence numbers. The link is treated as a black channel, one the endpoints do not trust, so each frame carries its own integrity and timing checks (section 4.4.1). The two paths share no processor cores, caches, or memory buses, and the learned proposers on the host have no memory-mapped input/output (MMIO) access to the drive registers. Candidate chunks cross the link into the MCU’s own SRAM through a double buffer. A receive DMA transfer fills a private receive buffer. The receive interrupt copies each checked frame into a single mailbox slot guarded by a sequence lock, or seqlock, whose protocol section 4.3.2 develops. A 1 kHz permission tick reads that slot, checks each proposal’s freshness, lease, and kinematic limits against measured encoder feedback, and is the only code that can send an admitted setpoint to the drives. The host is an unprivileged client; the microcontroller, with a short and cycle-counted loop, is the part whose worst-case execution time can be bounded.

Physical separation onto separate chips represents one realization. A single system-on-chip can also host both paths, provided that on-chip interconnects, cache partitioning, and memory controllers demonstrate equivalent response bounds and fault containment under stress. Choosing between discrete silicon and partitioned on-chip cores is the central placement trade-off, and until loaded experiments settle it, physical separation remains a foundational design hypothesis. Scheduling both paths on shared processor cores introduces the problem of mixed-criticality scheduling, in which each task carries execution-time bounds of graduated assurance levels. The operating system guarantees each deadline only under the bound its own criticality demands, which allows the proposer to overrun while the permission path executes deterministically (Vestal 2007). Sharing memory on a single die also demands explicit memory-bandwidth regulation to prevent tensor accelerators from starving real-time safety routines.

Vestal, Steve. 2007. “Preemptive Scheduling of Multi-Criticality Systems with Varying Degrees of Execution Time Assurance.” IEEE International Real-Time Systems Symposium (RTSS), 239–43. https://doi.org/10.1109/RTSS.2007.47.

↪ Mixed-criticality scheduling, memory-bandwidth regulation, and the other remedies for shared-die contention are developed in Silicon Isolation Decisions.

Designating an embedded controller as a safety microcontroller claims far more than physical separation on a circuit board. It denotes a device developed, qualified, and assessed under the IEC 61508 functional-safety standard, accompanied by a safety manual specifying its certified integrity level and the operational conditions that support depends on (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010b). At the silicon level, a safety MCU integrates specialized hardware redundancy to detect internal faults autonomously. Dual processor cores execute in lockstep while a comparator flags divergent execution. Error-correcting codes (ECC) protect internal SRAM and flash memories against single-event upsets. A hardware memory protection unit (MPU) confines each task to its assigned memory range. Analog monitors continuously watch the clock and supply voltages. Built-in self-tests run at power-up and periodically during operation to verify internal logic. An external watchdog timer, clocked by its own oscillator and serviced within a strict time window or through challenge-response messages, monitors the core. Because the watchdog relies on an independent clock source, a physical fault that stops the microcontroller’s oscillator cannot stall the watchdog, allowing it to detect that silent processor failure and command a qualified out-of-band response. Successful physical containment still depends on the response path, its surviving power, and the actuator premises.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2010a. IEC 61508:2010: Functional Safety of Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Organization for Standardization. 2023. ISO 13849-1:2023: Safety of Machinery, Safety-Related Parts of Control Systems, Part 1: General Principles for Design. International Organization for Standardization.

Two different protections meet at this microcontroller, and keeping them apart explains the standards that follow. The chunk lease protects the stopping budget against a proposer that stalls or misbehaves. The rated claim that the machine will not strike a person rests elsewhere, on the independent protective path of Delay consumes stopping clearance (a certified safety scanner wired to the drives, with its own sensing, timing, and power) and on the drives’ certified stop functions. Hardware diagnostic features alone do not confer a safety rating on the robot. Under functional safety standards, integrity belongs to a specific safety function, such as revoking actuator authority when a lease expires, rather than to an individual chip. The industrial standard IEC 61508 classifies safety functions by Safety Integrity Level (SIL 1 through SIL 4), where each numerical increment demands approximately an order-of-magnitude reduction in the allowable rate of dangerous failure (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010a). The machinery standard ISO 13849-1 grades safety functions by Performance Level (PL a through PL e) (International Organization for Standardization 2023). A Performance Level combines four architectural factors. The structural Category ranges from single-channel architectures using well-tried components (Categories B and 1), through single channels with periodic diagnostic testing (Category 2), to dual-channel redundant designs in which no single hardware fault defeats safety enforcement (Categories 3 and 4). Component reliability is quantified as the mean time to dangerous failure. Diagnostic coverage defines the fraction of dangerous failures detected by internal testing. Physical defenses guard against common-cause failure, where a shared disturbance disables redundant channels simultaneously. The safety microcontroller contributes certified failure rates and diagnostic coverage to this calculation, while the target Performance Level is dictated by the severity and exposure of the physical hazard the safety function must mitigate.

↪ The Performance Level each certified stop and speed function of the running machine must reach is developed in Certified safety functions beneath the permission path.

The physical architecture of the robot makes a deliberate engineering trade-off that functional safety certification must evaluate. Its permission path executes real-time performance tasks, validating trajectory proposals and interpolating admitted waypoints into drive setpoints, alongside the safety function intended for qualification: revoking actuator authority and commanding a safe stop when a chunk lease expires or the host heartbeat falls silent. The algorithmic admission checks themselves carry no formal safety rating, which is why the protective claim stays with the scanner and the drive stop circuits (Certified safety functions beneath the permission path). Standard functional-safety practice keeps complex control algorithms out of the certified safety channel, relying instead on an independent monitoring channel to satisfy Category 3 and Category 4 redundancy requirements. Modern motor drives supply part of that second channel through integrated hardware safety functions such as Safe Stop 1 (SS1), which brakes the motor under drive control and then removes torque.

↪ How Safe Stop 1 and the other drive safety functions realize the industrial stop categories is developed in Industrial stop categories and the safe torque off trap.

Any hardware split must keep the permission path the sole writer of the actuators and bound its check time across all expected hardware and software faults. Putting the two paths on separate chips is the cleanest way to start, though empirical timing evidence under load must still confirm that isolation. Separate processors, however, run on asynchronous clocks at very different rates, so the link between them must pass trajectory updates continuously without ever forcing the fast permission reader to wait on the slower host writer.

4.3 Multi-Rate Cadences

A motor drive cannot wait for the next camera-based proposal before regulating winding current. Conversely, forcing a learned policy to evaluate at current-loop rates wastes compute without generating fresher sensory observations. Decoupling these cadences allows each workload to meet its distinct physical or computational deadline, provided the boundary interface preserves data freshness and never blocks local real-time control.

4.3.1 Physical time constants and sampling phase lag

The mobile manipulator’s motor inverters regulate winding current every 50 μs (20 kHz), while its chunk policy requires 40 ms at p99 latency to ingest camera frames and generate an action chunk. No single cadence can serve both the winding’s electromagnetic dynamics and the cost of deep visual inference. The four tiers named in section 4.1 therefore each take their rate from a different time constant:

  1. Inner Commutation and Current Loop (20 kHz, 50 μs cycle): The PWM carrier frequency, selected to balance switching losses, current ripple, and acoustic noise, fixes the sampling instants. Phase currents are sampled once per carrier period at its midpoint, so a 20 kHz carrier yields \(\SamplingPeriod =\) 50 μs. The stator winding’s electrical time constant establishes the plant pole that the current regulator must compensate. For the hub motors, \(\ElectricalTimeConstant = \PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance =\) 2.7 ms (Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping) places that pole near 59 Hz. The closed-loop current bandwidth is tuned well above this pole and at least a factor of ten below the sampling rate, reaching at most about 2 kHz (Franklin et al. 1998). This loop executes directly within drive silicon and commands electromagnetic torque through the motor controller.
  2. Permission and Position-Supervision Tick (1 kHz, 1000 μs \(\pm\) 10 μs cycle): The rate here is set by distance rather than by a plant pole. Each tick of detection delay is base travel the stopping budget must absorb, and each tick must also supply the drives a fresh position setpoint. At one tick per millisecond both demands are met with the margins quantified below, while the drives close the subordinate velocity and current loops.
  3. Action-Chunk Policy Stream (20 Hz, 50 ms cycle): Bridging observation age and mechanical execution, the local policy emits receding-horizon action chunks (\(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 waypoints spaced 25 ms apart). On this machine, the policy is a compact visuomotor network following the Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) architecture, which decodes an entire chunk in a single forward pass. Action chunking priced it against the iterative diffusion and flow-matching heads that trade more passes for expressiveness. Each pass must stream the model’s weights from memory on the application processor’s accelerator, which caps the pass rate near \(\PolicyPassRate \le \SustainedMemoryBandwidth/\WeightMemory\). Because one pass yields all 16 setpoints, that sweep is paid once per chunk rather than once per setpoint, which is what lets the policy renew at this rate.
  4. Multimodal Intent Planner (2 Hz, 500 ms cycle): A high-capacity VLA model turns camera frames and a natural-language instruction into task goals and navigation targets. Its period is set by the prefill and weight sweeps of The memory wall, not by any plant dynamics. It may therefore steer the chunk policy but never hold a chunk lease, the authority to keep driving the actuators that section 4.4 sizes in tens of milliseconds.

The two proposer cadences reflect mainstream action-chunking and VLA architectures as of 2026, and their frequencies will shift as models and accelerators evolve. The method developed below does not depend on them. It anchors the lease’s lower limit to what the proposer can supply and its upper limit to the physical stopping budget.

The current-loop rate follows directly from the winding’s electrical time constant \(\PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\), and the loop closes within the drive’s local inverter. The harder timing question arises one level up, at the joint. There, inertia and structural compliance set the required closed-loop bandwidth, and every digital stage between sensor and actuator (sampling, computation, bus transport) exacts a penalty in phase lag. The crossover frequency \(\CrossoverFrequency\) marks the point where open-loop gain equals unity. At this frequency, the phase margin4 defines the maximum additional phase lag the system can absorb before feedback transitions from stabilizing attenuation to self-reinforcing oscillation.

4 Phase Margin: Bode (1945) introduced gain and phase margins to measure how far a feedback amplifier sits from instability, turning the stability criterion of Nyquist (1932) into a practical design margin. In cyber-physical loops, delay erodes the modeled phase reserve and can destabilize the loop under its nominal assumptions. Establishing actual damping and stability requires the complete sampled plant, compensation, and timing response. Because a pure transport or compute delay \(\PureTransportDelay\) subtracts \(\CrossoverFrequency \PureTransportDelay\) radians of phase regardless of cause, bus cycles, compute stages, and zero-order holds all draw from the same finite phase budget.

Bode, Hendrik W. 1945. Network Analysis and Feedback Amplifier Design. D. Van Nostrand Company.
Nyquist, Harry. 1932. “Regeneration Theory.” Bell System Technical Journal 11 (1): 126–47. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1932.tb02344.x.

Excess phase lag causes corrective control torques to arrive out of phase with physical joint motion, eventually turning stabilizing negative feedback into unstable positive feedback (Nyquist 1932). Closing a feedback loop across the permission tick accumulates three distinct sources of latency. The discrete sample-and-hold introduces an effective transport delay equal to half the sample period (\(\SamplingPeriod/2\)). The enforcer requires compute time (\(\EnforcerComputeTime\)) to evaluate constraints. The resulting command then incurs fieldbus transit latency (\(\CommandTransportTime\)) before reaching the drive. Because a pure delay \(\PureTransportDelay\) removes \(\AngularFrequency \PureTransportDelay\) radians of phase across all frequencies, these stages collectively erode the phase margin at crossover according to equation 1, the total-delay relation given in Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag: \[\DelayPhaseLag = \CrossoverFrequency\left(\frac{\SamplingPeriod}{2} + \EnforcerComputeTime + \CommandTransportTime\right) \tag{1}\]

Consider an illustrative joint-loop crossover of 20 Hz and a design phase erosion allowance of 5\(^\circ\). At a 20 Hz crossover, each 1 ms of pure delay along the loop spans that fraction of a full \(360^\circ\) oscillation cycle and costs 7.2\(^\circ\) of phase margin. A 1 ms tick’s sample-and-hold accounts for 3.6\(^\circ\), which fits comfortably within the budget. However, the 1 ms fieldbus transit cycle contributes 7.2\(^\circ\) on its own, and the enforcer’s declared worst-case execution time of 250 μs adds another 1.8\(^\circ\). The combined delay of 1.75 ms imposes 12.6\(^\circ\) of phase lag, 2.5× the allowance. If the controller delays output release until the subsequent tick boundary, total phase erosion escalates to 18\(^\circ\) over 2.5 ms. Because the phase budget tolerates at most 0.69 ms of total delay, less than a single bus cycle, no increase in tick frequency can keep a 20 Hz loop closed across this communication bus within its erosion allowance. The loop might remain stable, but it would spend phase margin the design never allocated to delay. Given 1.75 ms of unavoidable transit and processing latency, a 1 ms tick stays within the chosen phase erosion allowance only at crossover frequencies no greater than 7.9 Hz. Reducing the tick rate to \(500\text{ Hz}\) increases the sample-and-hold penalty alone to 7.2\(^\circ\), whereas driving the tick frequency toward infinity still leaves the bus transport penalty untouched. Loop placement, rather than clock scaling, resolves this architectural constraint.

The bus cycle alone spends more phase than the loop can spare.

Consequently, the machine closes its high-rate joint velocity and current loops directly within the decentralized motor drives, where co-located encoder sampling bypasses the fieldbus entirely. The centralized 1 ms permission tick operates strictly as a supervisor and trajectory setpoint generator. This decouples the tick from joint-level phase margin constraints, replacing them with two distinct systems requirements. First, as a safety supervisor, each millisecond of detection delay incurs 1.5 mm of unbraked base travel at maximum drive velocity, directly consuming clearance from the stopping budget (notebook 4.1). Second, as a setpoint source, the tick must evaluate the admitted trajectory frequently enough to supply smooth kinematic references to the drives. Transport delay along this reference path introduces tracking lag rather than eroding the drive loop’s phase margin, because the reference sits outside the feedback loop. Any outer supervisory corrections closed across the bus, such as cartesian compliance trims, must restrict their crossover bandwidth below 7.9 Hz.

Closing those loops in the drive is a hardware arrangement rather than merely a firmware setting. The fastest loop sits beside its sensor and its switch, so phase current is measured and switched next to the winding with no host processor in the loop. Dedicated PWM timers in the same silicon enforce the gap between complementary switch transitions that keeps the power bridge from shorting.5 The running machine’s brushless hub drives follow this arrangement at a larger scale; the small stepper-motor driver of figure 3 shows it on one chip.

5 Dead-Time Delay: The interval in which both switches of a half-bridge leg are off, so the leg never shorts the DC bus, is the inverter dead time introduced in Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping. Dedicated pulse-width modulation timers insert it in hardware (\(100\text{--}500\text{ ns}\)) on every transition, and drive firmware applies online volt-second compensation for the voltage error it introduces.

Perception pipelines feeding the policy stream execute at intermediate cadences constrained by optical physics, sensor exposure durations, and relative scene velocity. In a typical \(300\text{ lux}\) warehouse lighting environment requiring a 15 ms exposure, the robot base traveling at its 1.5 m/s drive limit covers \(\ExposureTravelDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\,\ExposureDuration =\) 22.5 mm while the shutter is open. Resulting image-plane blur interacts with scene depth and optical geometry, setting physical limits on feature tracking. The achievable signal-to-noise ratio, spatial motion blur, and image preprocessing latency jointly establish the feasible sensing cadence for visual control, a trade-off Sensor Perception develops. For the permission path the exposure matters as time. An observation is dated at its exposure midpoint, so half the exposure is already part of its age before any processing begins, and that age is the first interval the lease budget must charge (table 1).

The chunk-policy generation rate must satisfy the proposal-supply budget set in Action chunking. For our mobile manipulator, a 20 Hz renewal cycle paired with an \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 waypoint horizon (Zhao et al. 2023) bridges visual perception and high-rate actuation. The 50 ms renewal period matches 2 discrete setpoint steps of 25 ms, so every scheduled renewal lands on a setpoint boundary, provided the handoff between the two clocks never makes the faster reader wait.

Zhao, Tony Z., Vikash Kumar, Sergey Levine, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation with Low-Cost Hardware.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.016.

4.3.2 Lock-free and wait-free mailbox exchanges

If a 1 kHz safety loop blocks on a mutual exclusion lock held by a 20 Hz neural network runtime, one page fault or scheduling hiccup in the runtime becomes an unbounded priority inversion, and the hard real-time controller misses its cycle deadline. Because the tiers run on different processors at different rates, every exchange across a rate boundary must give the faster reader bounded, nonblocking access. Panel (b) of figure 4 follows the remedy developed here, the mailbox, through a clean exchange, a write preempted by the tick, and a stalled host, and in none of the three does the reader wait. Panel (a), which shows what the tick does when the proposer stops renewing altogether, returns in section 4.4 once the lease that governs it is defined.

Standard inter-process communication libraries rarely provide deterministic execution bounds out of the box. Message serialization, dynamic memory allocation, intermediate queueing, and thread scheduling can each introduce unpredictable delivery stalls. While shared memory eliminates buffer copying and kernel context switches, it requires concurrency controls to prevent the reader from ingesting partially written, inconsistent command payloads. The communication protocol must strictly bound the consumer’s execution time, even when the producer stalls at the most unfavorable point in its write cycle.

While a first-in, first-out (FIFO) queue guarantees sequential message delivery, message ordering is the wrong semantic model for physical motion control. If the control consumer falls behind the rate of production, queued motion plans represent progressively stale physical state estimates. Backpressure, the queue’s way of slowing a producer, cannot pause moving mass. The real-time enforcer requires the freshest complete proposal, paired with a strict temporal deadline. This principle does not prevent the controller from buffering the future waypoints of an already admitted trajectory; those waypoints follow an explicit execution schedule governed by a valid lease. Rather, it prevents treating the delayed consumption of historical commands as valid control inputs. Real-time distributed systems distinguish these two communication semantics. An event message is queued and consumed exactly once, where dropping a message loses state history. A state message represents current environmental state and updates in place, allowing new observations to overwrite older ones without queuing (Kopetz 2011). The proposal mailbox implements state-message semantics via a single-slot seqlock, following the non-blocking write protocol of Kopetz and Reisinger (1993) to ensure mutual independence between writer progress and reader execution.

A baseline nonblocking exchange uses a single-producer single-consumer (SPSC) seqlock mailbox6 in the microcontroller’s own static memory. The proposal first crosses the board-level link as a framed packet, which a DMA transfer lands in a private receive buffer. A receive interrupt outside the tick checks the frame’s CRC and sequence number and copies the proposal into the mailbox slot. Both participants therefore execute on the microcontroller, the receive interrupt as writer and the permission tick as reader, and the application processor never touches mailbox memory.

6 Versioned Read: Lamport (1977) showed that a reader can detect a concurrent, overlapping write without acquiring mutual exclusion locks. By incrementing a version counter before and after updating payload data, and having the reader sample the counter in reverse order, any mismatch reliably flags a torn read.

Lamport, Leslie. 1977. “Concurrent Reading and Writing.” Communications of the ACM 20 (11): 806–11. https://doi.org/10.1145/359863.359878.

↪ The boundary contract that keeps the application processor out of the microcontroller’s mailbox memory is developed in The Boundary Contract.

The writer increments the sequence counter to an odd value, copies the checked frame into the slot, and increments the counter to an even value using store-release memory ordering. The reader makes exactly one bounded read attempt per control cycle and rejects the snapshot if the counter begins odd or changes before the read finishes. A snapshot that passes becomes the active chunk only after its integrity, freshness, and physical-admissibility checks succeed, and the tick then interpolates it into setpoints (section 4.4.2). A rejected read leaves the previously admitted chunk in force, so the reader never blocks on the writer. Because the timer tick has higher priority than the receive interrupt, a tick that arrives mid-write sees the odd counter and discards the torn read in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) time without spinning. It keeps executing the active chunk for as long as its lease and evidence remain valid. A crashed or stalled application processor cannot leave the counter odd, because it never writes the slot. Its failure appears only as an absence of incoming frames, and the active chunk’s lease expires on schedule.

Formally, the reader validates the snapshot using two boundary counter observations: \[\ReadStartSequenceCount \equiv \ReadEndSequenceCount \equiv 0 \pmod 2 \quad \text{and} \quad \ReadStartSequenceCount = \ReadEndSequenceCount,\] where an odd count signals an active write and a value mismatch signals preemption during the copy. Unlike mutexes or semaphores, this protocol never suspends either participant. Because the reader never retries within a single tick, its computational overhead remains strictly \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) regardless of writer activity. Correct execution requires that memory loads occur in strict programmatic order. Within a single core arbitrating between an interrupt handler and the periodic tick, compiler optimization barriers prevent instruction reordering. However, when a DMA engine or a second processor core writes the slot directly, hardware memory barriers become mandatory. On Arm Cortex-M profile cores, this requires the full-system data memory barrier instruction (DMB) (ARM Limited 2012). Omitting this barrier allows out-of-order execution pipelines to speculatively load payload fields before validating the sequence counter, silently allowing torn data into the control loop.

ARM Limited. 2012. ARM Cortex-M Programming Guide to Memory Barrier Instructions. Application Note 321, ARM DAI 0321A. ARM Limited.

7 Wait-Free: Herlihy (1991) defined an implementation as wait-free when every operation finishes in a bounded number of its own steps, whatever the other processes do. That is stronger than lock-free progress, which guarantees only that some operation completes while a particular one may retry indefinitely. The one-attempt seqlock read is bounded but can come back empty-handed; the slot mailbox is bounded and always returns a complete snapshot, and the extra slot is the price of that second property.

Herlihy, Maurice. 1991. “Wait-Free Synchronization.” ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems 13 (1): 124–49. https://doi.org/10.1145/114005.102808.

A wait-free three-slot mailbox7 can avoid read/write collisions by assigning separate slots to the writer, the published proposal, and the reader. Atomic publication transfers slot ownership; it must never let the writer overwrite the reader’s active slot. Unlike the one-attempt seqlock, this design can provide a complete published snapshot without discarding a collided read. Its extra storage and ownership protocol must still be checked on the target hardware. Simpson’s four-slot mechanism reaches the same guarantee, a complete snapshot for a reader that never waits, using only single-bit control variables written with ordinary stores and no atomic read-modify-write instruction (Simpson 1990). Neither design makes an old proposal fresh or extends its lease.

Sequential diagnostic telemetry, by contrast, operates under event semantics. A single-producer single-consumer (SPSC) ring buffer preserves an ordered log of events until its bounded capacity fills, whereas the proposal mailbox exposes only the latest complete candidate state. The permission interface relies on these bounded-access guarantees to eliminate wait states; it intentionally discards historical command queues.

↪ The memory-ordering primitives, cache-line padding, and slot-ownership protocols that implement both exchanges are developed in Heterogeneous SoC Mailboxes, Memory Barriers, and Zero-Copy IPC.

Macenski, Steven, Tully Foote, Brian Gerkey, Chris Lalancette, and William Woodall. 2022. “Robot Operating System 2: Design, Architecture, and Uses in the Wild.” Science Robotics 7 (66): eabm6074. https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.abm6074.
Object Management Group. 2015. Data Distribution Service (DDS), Version 1.4. Object Management Group.
Casini, Daniel, Tobias Blaß, Ingo Lütkebohle, and Björn B. Brandenburg. 2019. “Response-Time Analysis of ROS 2 Processing Chains Under Reservation-Based Scheduling.” 31st Euromicro Conference on Real-Time Systems (ECRTS 2019), Leibniz international proceedings in informatics (LIPIcs), vol. 133: 6:1–23. https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ECRTS.2019.6.
ros2_control Development Team. 2025. Realtime_tools: Tools for Hard Real-Time Threads in Ros2_control. GitHub repository.

No robotics middleware setting can stand in for the chunk lease, because none derives its parameters from the physical stopping budget and none executes on the consumer’s own hardware clock at the point of actuation. Middleware can still approximate the mailbox’s state-message semantics, and the mapping shows how far configuration goes. In ROS 2, communication relies on the Data Distribution Service (DDS) or equivalent publish-subscribe transports (Macenski et al. 2022), whose Quality-of-Service (QoS) policies reflect real-time contracts only partially (Object Management Group 2015). Configuring a keep-last history depth of one provides state-message semantics by retaining only the freshest sample. Selecting best-effort reliability drops lost packets rather than triggering retransmissions, avoiding the burst of stale samples that reliable delivery releases after network blips. A DDS deadline policy raises notification events when sample inter-arrival times exceed a threshold, acting as an alerting heartbeat without enforcing authority revocation. Similarly, a DDS lifespan policy purges samples whose age exceeds a configured threshold, but this mechanism relies on synchronized network clocks, defaults to arrival timestamps when synchronization degrades, and controls only message transport rather than granted actuation authority. Callback scheduling within middleware executors also introduces variable dispatch jitter, requiring dedicated response-time analyses across end-to-end processing chains (Casini et al. 2019). At the direct hardware interface, the ros2_control framework implements a nonblocking realtime buffer using a single try-lock attempt. If the writer holds the mutex, the read aborts and the controller retains its prior command (ros2_control Development Team 2025), which mimics the one-attempt semantics of a seqlock. Regardless of middleware abstractions, the physical permission path must independently enforce its own freshness checks and lease bounds.

↪ The inverse failure, middleware that reports healthy delivery while transmitting stale payload data, is developed in Spatial Belief Failure Modes and Map Corruption.

Because the receive interrupt write routine is brief and resumes immediately after the tick completes, an access collision discards at most one read attempt without delaying the tick. However, if the upstream link halts completely, the mailbox slot remains unchanged across thousands of consecutive ticks. Bounded reader execution time does not guarantee bounded publication latency. The system therefore requires an explicit physical policy governing how long a previously admitted motion chunk may continue commanding actuators. Under the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), every millisecond that a stalled proposal retains actuation authority produces physical displacement that cannot be retracted. That exposure has to be priced against the stopping budget, which makes the length of a proposal’s authority the next design decision.

4.4 Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget

A learned policy runtime can stall for many reasons: a page fault, thermal throttling on the vision accelerator, memory fragmentation, or an uncaught exception in perception preprocessing. The robot does not stop when its software does. A loaded base keeps its momentum (\(\LinearMomentum = \Mass \SpeedAtDetection\)) and covers 1.5 mm every millisecond at the drive limit, while the downstream controller keeps converting the remaining waypoints of the last admitted chunk into setpoints. To keep an unrenewed chunk from driving the machine into an obstacle, the permission path must end that chunk’s authority on its own hardware clock, whether or not the proposer ever reports its failure. How long it should wait before doing so is a question about distance, not software.

Definition 4.2: Chunk lease

Chunk lease is the interval, counted on the permission path’s own clock from a chunk’s admission, during which that admitted chunk may keep driving the actuators without a successful replacement; when it expires, the permission path ends the chunk’s authority and commands the state-matched fallback, a stop profile chosen from the machine’s measured speed and pose.

  1. Significance: The lease enters the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) of the stopping budget, so each millisecond of it costs \(\SpeedAtDetection\) of clear distance, 1.5 mm at the running machine’s drive limit. The machine’s 60 ms lease, with the tick and bus cycle that detect and deliver its expiry, adds 93 mm to the stopping budget (notebook 4.1).
  2. Distinction: Unlike a heartbeat timeout, which shows only that the host is alive, a lease is renewed only by a freshly admitted proposal. Its length is derived from the plant rather than configured: above the proposer’s renewal floor and below the stopping budget’s ceiling. Unlike an evidence deadline, it bounds how long one motion segment may execute, not how long the observation behind it stays valid.
  3. Common pitfall: Lengthening the lease to ride out proposer hiccups. A longer lease buys continuity with clearance, and a lease that reaches the pre-brake ceiling commits the machine to motion it can no longer stop in time.

In classical distributed systems, a lease grants bounded access to a shared digital resource, such as a distributed lock or cache entry, expiring automatically if the leaseholder crashes (Gray and Cheriton 1989). In physical AI, the leased resource is the kinetic authority to move mass through space. Because physical motion cannot be undone, the duration of a lease must be formally derived from the physical stopping budget. The distance covered during the lease, the detection latency, the fieldbus transit, and the brake onset, together with the braking distance itself, must fit within the available clearance \(\ClearDistance\) after the localization uncertainty and protective margin are subtracted. Rearranging the stopping-distance relation of equation 2.2, \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay + \SpeedAtDetection^2/2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin \le \ClearDistance\), and dividing the remaining pre-braking distance by vehicle velocity yields the pre-brake ceiling, representing the maximum tolerable delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) before physical braking deceleration must commence: \[\PrebrakeDelay \le \frac{\ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin - \SpeedAtDetection^2/2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{\SpeedAtDetection} \tag{2}\] where \(\SpeedAtDetection\) represents operating velocity, \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) denotes verified braking deceleration, and \(\LocalizationBound\) and \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\) represent the localization uncertainty and safety clearance of that formulation. As operating speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) decreases, the required braking distance shrinks quadratically while the velocity divisor decreases linearly, causing the pre-brake ceiling to expand toward infinity at rest. At maximum operating speed, however, every millisecond allocated to the lease path consumes exactly \(\SpeedAtDetection\) units of stopping clearance.

Panel (a) of figure 4 draws the lease on the running machine’s own clocks. The chunk policy renews twice and then stalls. The tick keeps executing the last admitted chunk only until the lease counted from that admission lapses, and the next tick, one bus cycle, and the brake onset then bring the fallback stop. Notebook 4.1 evaluates equation 2 for the mobile manipulator at its drive limit and prices that sequence inside it.

Napkin Math 4.1: The price of a lease
Problem: In the illustrative aisle scenario, the warehouse mobile manipulator cruises at its drive limit toward a person who has stepped out from a rack end and stopped. The permission path admits one last chunk and then receives no admissible replacement. How much time remains before braking must begin, and how much of it, and of the clear distance, does the chunk lease spend?

Variables:

  • Speed \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.5 m/s, the drive limit (illustrative); braking deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s², loaded on a dry floor (illustrative).
  • Clear distance \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (illustrative), less localization error \(\LocalizationBound =\) 50 mm (illustrative) and protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin =\) 100 mm (chosen).
  • Brake onset \(\ActuatorResponseTime =\) 20 ms (illustrative); permission tick \(\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1 ms (chosen); bus cycle \(\CommandTransportTime =\) 1 ms (illustrative).
  • Chunk lease \(\ChunkLease =\) 60 ms; renewal period \(\ChunkRenewalPeriod =\) 50 ms with a 5 ms variation allowance \(\RenewalJitterAllowance\) (all chosen).

Math: The braking term and the ceiling of equation 2: \[\frac{v^2}{2a_{\text{brake}}} = \frac{(1.5\ \text{m/s})^2}{2 \times 2\ \text{m/s}^2} = 562.5\ \text{mm}\] \[\tau_{\text{delay}} \le \frac{1100 - 150 - 562.5\ \text{mm}}{1.5\ \text{m/s}} = 258.3\ \text{ms}\] The stopping-budget terms that Kinetic Momentum already charged (braking, the localization and clearance overheads, and onset travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\ActuatorResponseTime =\) 30 mm) hold 742.5 mm of the clear distance. The lease path, lease plus the tick that notices its expiry plus the bus cycle that delivers the stop, is the next term: \[v\,(T_{\text{lease}} + T_{\text{tick}} + T_{\text{bus}}) = 1.5\ \text{m/s} \times (60 + 1 + 1)\ \text{ms} = 93\ \text{mm}\] \[742.5 + 93 = 835.5\ \text{mm}, \quad 1100 - 835.5 = 264.5\ \text{mm}\] In time, from the last admission to brake onset, and against the ceiling: \[62 + 20 = 82\ \text{ms}, \quad 258.3 - 82 = 176.3\ \text{ms}\] Continuous tracking also sets a floor under the lease: \[T_{\text{chunk}} + T_{\text{jitter}} = 50 + 5 = 55\ \text{ms} < T_{\text{lease}} = 60\ \text{ms}\]

Systems insight: A chunk lease is a term in the stopping budget, not a timeout chosen for convenience. Each millisecond added to it costs 1.5 mm of clear distance at the drive limit, distance that the observation age and the stop profile still need, so the lease sits just above its renewal floor rather than anywhere under the ceiling.

The renewal floor is the lease duration needed to cover the budgeted inter-arrival interval between successful admissions without starving trajectory execution. Because this floor cannot rely solely on the model’s 99th-percentile (\(\text{p99}\)) inference latency, it must accommodate bus transit, validation overhead, and mailbox synchronization jitter. If a renewal fails to arrive before the lease expires, the system terminates trajectory tracking rather than granting an unearned extension. The 176.3 ms remaining under the pre-brake ceiling is not spare margin either. It must still absorb the observation’s age at admission, which section 4.9.1 charges against it, and the jerk limits of the braking profile.

Every charge in this budget is an interval between the four boundary events introduced in The end-to-end sense-to-actuation latency pipeline. Capture dates an observation at its exposure midpoint, dispatch hands that evidence to the proposer, admission accepts a chunk built on it and starts the chunk’s lease, and brake onset marks the first physical braking force at the drives. In the worst-case stall scenario, an obstacle enters the path just after capture of the camera frame that produces the last healthy chunk, which therefore travels from capture through dispatch to admission without reflecting the hazard. When the subsequent renewal stalls, the robot continues tracking that last admitted chunk from admission until its lease expires and brake onset engages, chaining all three intervals end to end. Between these four events lie three intervals, each derived in exactly one chapter (table 1). Only row 3, the stalled-proposer response, is derived here, from the lease notebook. Rows 1 and 2 enter Part I as allocations: the observation age that The latency waterfall will build stage by stage, and the dispatch-to-admission interval that The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval will charge to the board link and the validator. Later chapters charge time against these events and this table rather than keeping their own stage lists.

The table also shows why the stopping budget and the lease rule charge different ages. The stopping budget’s pre-brake delay sums rows 1 and 3 and leaves row 2 to be paid from the spare that the elementary budget of Delay consumes stopping clearance left. The reason is that row 2’s length depends on where the proposer and the permission path run, a decision The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval makes. A renewal cannot defer that payment. A replacement chunk spends row 2 before its lease even starts, so the evidence age that the lease rule of section 4.9.1 charges at admission is rows 1 and 2 together.

4.4.1 Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts

Granting a fresh lease at admission without charging the time the observation already spent in inference and board-link transit lets the drives keep tracking a trajectory whose sensory evidence is already stale. Waiting on a single timeout while a hung policy thread sits inside a live host kernel lets them keep tracking a trajectory whose producer has stopped updating. Evidence keeps aging from capture onward, and admitting a chunk starts its lease on the microcontroller’s clock without rolling that age back. Neither the dispatch timestamp nor the newly granted lease accounts for the dispatch-to-admission interval, so the evidence-age check must charge row 2 of table 1 explicitly. Authority over an admitted chunk then ends at the earliest of four events: its evidence freshness deadline passes, its lease expires without a successful replacement, the host heartbeat times out, or an independent safety reflex such as the protective scanner path detects an obstacle. None of these waits for the others, and which fires first depends on the scene. At the conveyor, the tracked evidence horizon that Two proposers and the evidence horizon computed is several times longer than the lease, so there a stalled chunk loses its authority to the lease. The chunk lease bounds how long one admitted motion segment may execute. How long the strategic goal stays meaningful is a separate grant, the intent lease of The Intent Lease Specification and Contract, and neither is the nominal planning horizon.

↩︎ The freshness deadline that bounds how old evidence may be at actuation is established in Measurement Freshness.

Three independent supervisory timers guard the exchange, and each monitors a distinct failure domain:

  1. The chunk lease (\(\ChunkLease =\) 60 ms): Bounds how long an admitted proposal may command actuators. Only a freshly validated, admitted proposal chunk renews the lease. Its duration is derived directly from the physical stopping budget, sitting between the renewal floor and the pre-brake ceiling (notebook 4.1).
  2. The host heartbeat (\(\HostHeartbeatRate =\) 100 Hz, timeout 30 ms): Asserts that the application processor operating system kernel and user-space runtime remain alive. Expiry of this timeout revokes the active proposal without waiting for the chunk lease. Heartbeat pulses never renew the chunk lease, because a live process can easily suffer algorithmic livelock or produce corrupted, out-of-distribution setpoints.
  3. The microcontroller self-watchdog (\(\SelfWatchdogTimeout =\) 5 ms): Monitors whether the permission path completes its required checks within the watchdog interval. The permission loop feeds this hardware watchdog only upon successfully completing all verification checks in a tick; a hung enforcer trips the watchdog even if timer interrupts continue firing (section 4.10).

A watchdog refresh confirms only that the specific task servicing the timer is still executing. In Bookout v. Toyota (2013), litigation centered on whether a timer-tick interrupt could continue petting a watchdog while application-level control tasks had hung (Barr 2013). Although federal investigations by NASA and NHTSA identified no electronic mechanism capable of causing large unintended accelerations (NASA Engineering and Safety Center 2011), the dispute highlights a real-time principle. Watchdog refresh points must be coupled to the completion of safety-critical algorithmic cycles rather than independent periodic interrupts.

Barr, Michael. 2013. Bookout v. Toyota Motor Corp.: 2005 Camry L4 Software Analysis.
NASA Engineering and Safety Center. 2011. Technical Support to the NHTSA on the Reported Toyota Motor Corporation (TMC) Unintended Acceleration Investigation. National Aeronautics; Space Administration.

Real-time safety requires wrapping the proposer’s payload in a transport and authority envelope. The system enforces chunk leases through the proposal header, a structured wire contract that enables the permission path to validate candidate trajectories without placing trust in host application software. The header encapsulates a monotonically increasing sequence number and the evidence timestamp of the source sensory frame (including clock conversion error bounds). It also carries the host dispatch timestamp and the requested lease duration (which the permission path caps at the lease it derives, so a proposer can ask for less authority but never more). A cryptographic parent-record digest supports provenance tracking. The remaining fields are the coordinate frame identifier, the payload type enum, and a 32-bit cyclic redundancy check (CRC-32). For the default payload type the payload consists of \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) kinematic waypoints specifying joint coordinates and velocities. The payload-type field is where other command forms announce themselves, so an impedance target (section 4.6.1) carries its stiffness and damping gains beside its equilibrium waypoints. In this layered architecture, the learned policy expresses kinematic intent, the permission enforcer converts admitted waypoints into position setpoints, and the drives regulate joint velocity and motor current. The payload deliberately omits raw torque commands; while direct-torque policies require alternate wire representations and specialized admission checks, their outputs remain untrusted proposals. In all cases, an admitted proposal retains execution authority only until the earlier of its lease expiration and its evidence freshness deadline, regardless of whether cancellation packets arrive across the network.

↩︎ The proposal payload, the candidate motion and the evidence times it carries, is established in Information the proposer must supply.

EtherCAT Technology Group. 2019. Safety over EtherCAT (FSoE): Introduction and Overview. EtherCAT Technology Group seminar slides.

Industrial safety networking adopted the same transport safeguards decades before the advent of learned robotic proposers. Functional-safety fieldbus standards such as IEC 61784-3 establish the black-channel principle. Transmission media, network controllers, and intermediate switches are treated as untrusted, fallible channels, which requires safety-critical packets to encapsulate self-contained integrity and timing protections verified exclusively at communication endpoints (International Electrotechnical Commission 2021). Fieldbus profiles like PROFIsafe and Safety over EtherCAT (FSoE) exemplify this architecture. FSoE protects frames against corruption using CRCs, against repetition and packet loss using sequence counters, against misrouting using unique connection identifiers, and against transit delay using watchdog timers. Missing or delayed frames force the receiver into a deterministic safe state (EtherCAT Technology Group 2019). The proposal header mirrors this design field for field: checksums guard integrity, sequence numbers detect drops, payload identifiers ensure proper dispatch, and the chunk lease replaces the watchdog timer. The physical link between host application processor and safety microcontroller operates as an untrusted black channel; end-to-end header checks allow the permission path to enforce its declared physical admission contract without trusting host operating system integrity. Classical real-time theory provides the complementary semantic foundation. Kopetz defines a computing system’s internal representation of a dynamic physical entity as a real-time image, which remains temporally accurate only during a bounded physical duration following the initial observation (Kopetz 2011). The proposal’s evidence deadline represents this temporal accuracy window applied to sensory inputs, while the chunk lease bounds the duration of derived actuation. This architecture adds one thing to these precedents, the grounding of temporal validity. Whereas industrial fieldbuses configure watchdog timeouts empirically and real-time images reflect passive plant dynamics, the chunk lease is derived analytically from the physical stopping budget above and the proposer’s renewal floor below (equation 2, notebook 4.1).

↪ Byte layout: The proposal header’s and chunk payload’s fields, types, and offsets are given in Proposal header and chunk payload.

Every record crossing the proposal boundary expresses temporal quantities referenced to the safety microcontroller’s monotonic clock. Upstream producers operating on independent oscillator domains map timestamps using a versioned affine transformation and transmit explicit synchronization error bounds, which the safety consumer adds to all age evaluations. Wall-clock calendar time is restricted to diagnostic logging and software release expiration; it never enters real-time safety gating.

Individual boundary records guard against three orthogonal failure modes through three distinct mechanisms, only two of them cryptographic. A checksum across the header and payload, a CRC rather than a cryptographic function, detects transmission bit flips and memory corruption during transit, leaving origin authentication unaddressed. Similarly, a cryptographic digest of the parent record establishes computational lineage without authenticating the sender. Cryptographic message authentication codes (MACs) appear exclusively where sender identity conveys explicit operational authority: network teleoperation commands, immutable authority audit logs, and release artifacts verified during deployment. Learned model proposals omit cryptographic signatures entirely, because the permission enforcer validates candidates based on physical admissibility and temporal freshness rather than origin. A cryptographic key stored inside an untrusted host environment provides no assurance of kinematic safety.

↪ How a teleoperation command’s authentication tag is verified and priced against the fallback deadline is developed in Authenticating an Intervention.

The reader’s possible failures fall into four classes (table 2), and none of them lets a failed producer make the permission path wait. The classes keep the exchange from blocking. They do not by themselves contain the plant, because the fallback each one selects still needs bounded timing and a validated stop. Even a valid admitted chunk still has to be stepped from the proposer’s coarse waypoint spacing down to the permission tick’s rate.

4.4.2 Rate interpolation and boundary fallback execution

Holding each admitted waypoint constant for its full 25 ms spacing until the next waypoint arrives injects staircase position steps into the drives rather than a smooth trajectory, delaying reference tracking by half the step spacing on average and exciting high-frequency structural resonances. Bridging the learned policy’s renewal rate (20 Hz) and the real-time permission tick (1 ms) without those disturbances requires guarding against three sampled-data failure modes: reference lag, spectral aliasing, and buffer starvation. Because the zero-order hold’s latency sits entirely on the feedforward reference path outside the feedback loop, it appears as tracking error rather than eroding feedback phase margins (section 4.3.1). Confusing reference lag with closed-loop phase lag often leads engineers to misdiagnose tracking errors as plant instability. To eliminate discrete steps across chunk boundaries, the permission enforcer constructs a quintic Hermite spline bridge matching boundary position, velocity, and acceleration. These six boundary constraints guarantee \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) continuity across chunk transitions, meaning position, velocity, and acceleration all stay continuous. Evaluating this admitted spline at 1 kHz yields continuous position references, smooth velocity feeds, and feedforward acceleration profiles for the underlying joint controllers.

↩︎ Action chunking, which produces the trajectory segments this interpolator densifies, is established in Action chunking.

8 Filter Group Delay: Digital smoothing filters commonly applied in machine learning pipelines introduce significant phase lag when placed inside feedback control loops. An \(\FilterTapCount\)-tap moving-average finite impulse response (FIR) filter imposes an average group delay of \((\FilterTapCount-1)/2\) sample intervals. Consequently, a 9-tap moving average operating at the 1 ms permission tick delays feedback signals by 4 ms. At the illustrative 20 Hz crossover frequency from section 4.3.1, this delay burns 28.8\(^\circ\) of phase, 5.8× the design allowance. In real-time control paths, decimation and filter topologies must be selected for minimal phase lag rather than cosmetic smoothing.

Spectral aliasing occurs when unmodeled high-frequency mechanical vibration or sensor noise, sampled too slowly, reappears as a false low-frequency signal in the slower streams that estimators and the tick consume. The architecture mitigates aliasing through front-end analog filtering followed by multi-rate digital decimation,8 low-pass filtering a fast sample stream before keeping only one sample in several, within the high-rate microcontroller domain. Classical sampled-data control models assume constant, deterministic sampling periods. Temporal jitter, a sampling interval that varies from cycle to cycle, degrades phase margin more severely than a constant delay of equivalent mean duration (Cervin et al. 2003). Discrete trajectory execution also assumes setpoints replenish continuously before downstream buffers drain. The permission path enforces these invariants via cycle-deterministic hardware timer interrupts and real-time buffer-depth accounting. If the upstream planner stalls and the trajectory buffer reaches its terminal waypoint, the enforcer rejects stale extrapolation and commands a validated local stopping profile.

An overrun costs something different at each of the four tiers:

  • Current loop (20 kHz): Its jitter allowance is below \(10\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) (table 3). A timing overrun, gate-drive desynchronization, or current spike trips a hardware fault line that disables the inverter’s PWM stages.
  • Permission tick (1 kHz): A deadline overrun by the enforcer software sends the machine to the local deceleration stop.
  • Chunk policy (20 Hz): Inference latency is stochastic (40 ms at p99), so some chunks arrive late.
