A late chunk costs nothing until the active chunk’s lease or evidence deadline runs out, and the enforcer never waits on the proposer’s optimistic estimate of when it will finish. When either deadline passes, the enforcer commands the state-matched fallback.
  • Intent planner (2 Hz): Its goals expire under a separate intent lease, a longer grant of authority over the goal itself rather than over any motion (developed in The Intent Lease Specification and Contract). When it lapses, the system holds station instead of chasing a stale goal.

The lease notebook charged the tick and the bus cycle as fixed terms, 1 ms and 1 ms, as though each were certain to finish on time. That premise is the next thing to establish. It requires a worst-case response bound for every stage between the mailbox and the drives, and no benchmark or faster processor substitutes for one.

4.5 Real-Time Command Transport and Bus Determinism

A profiling campaign can run the permission tick for days without a missed deadline and still not establish the tick’s worst case. An empirical percentile says how often sampled executions stayed under a threshold. It says nothing about executions that meet a combination of competing tasks, memory contention, or peripheral delay the campaign never produced. The lease budget needs the other kind of number: a response-time bound that accounts for every unit of permitted computation and queuing delay along the critical path under defined operating conditions. In an embodied system, timing determinism protects both stability and safety, because delayed feedback erodes the controller’s phase margin and delayed actuation consumes stopping clearance before the chassis can decelerate. Bounding the tick on the microcontroller comes first; bounding the drive network that carries its admitted setpoints to the motors follows the same logic (Kopetz 2011).

4.5.1 Response time analysis and execution variance

A permission tick whose own instructions finish in 250 μs can still miss its 400 μs deadline if a lower-priority link driver masks interrupts while re-arming a DMA descriptor or a burst of higher-priority timer interrupts preempts the tick mid-cycle. Response Time Analysis (RTA) bounds a task’s total response time by summing the three constituents introduced in section 4.2: execution time, blocking delay, and preemption interference (Joseph and Pandya 1986; Audsley et al. 1993). To guarantee schedulability, each term requires an analytical upper bound under specified operating conditions: \[\WorstCaseResponseTime_i = \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i + \TaskBlockingTime_i + \TaskInterferenceTime_i \le \TaskRelativeDeadline_i \tag{3}\] where \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\) is the worst-case execution time (WCET) of task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\), \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) is the maximum blocking time spent waiting for lower-priority tasks to vacate shared critical sections, \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) is the maximum interference from preempting higher-priority tasks, timer interrupts, and DMA transfers, and \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i\) is the relative deadline.

Each term demands distinct evidentiary backing. Formal static timing analysis against an accurate cycle-level processor model can establish a bound on the WCET \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i\). High-watermark profiling under stress-bench conditions instead characterizes observed execution times; it can refute a declared WCET but cannot establish one. The blocking term \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) represents the longest critical section of any lower-priority task that can lock a shared resource required by task \(\RealTimeTaskIndex\). The priority ceiling protocol establishes a provable bound on \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\). In this protocol, each synchronization lock carries a priority ceiling equal to the highest priority of any task that may acquire it. A task may acquire a lock only if its priority strictly exceeds the priority ceilings of all locks currently held by other tasks. Furthermore, any lock holder that blocks a higher-priority task temporarily inherits that task’s elevated priority. Under these constraints, a task is blocked by at most a single lower-priority critical section across its entire execution (Sha et al. 1990). An immediate priority ceiling protocol (IPCP), which instantly elevates a lock holder to the resource ceiling upon acquisition, guarantees the same blocking bound with lower runtime overhead. When a real-time task avoids shared software mutexes altogether through lock-free data structures, the blocking term \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) reduces to the longest interval during which lower-priority driver code masks processor interrupts or holds a non-preemptible hardware peripheral transaction. Finally, the interference term \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) aggregates the execution time of all higher-priority tasks and interrupt service routines released during the response window. Because a longer response time provides a wider window for higher-priority tasks to re-trigger, calculating \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\) requires solving a fixed-point recurrence relation. Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability details this formulation. Equation 3 defines the overarching budget that the recurrence resolves, and notebook 4.2 evaluates that budget for the permission tick.

Napkin Math 4.2: The response-time margin
Problem: The permission tick must finish within its deadline after every release. With its declared WCET and illustrative bounds on blocking and interference, does it meet the deadline, and what happens to the margin when one term loses its bound?

Variables:

  • Declared WCET \(\WorstCaseExecutionTime_i =\) 250 μs and deadline \(\TaskRelativeDeadline_i =\) 400 μs (both chosen).
  • Blocking \(\TaskBlockingTime_i =\) 50 μs, the longest section in which lower-priority code masks the tick, such as the link driver re-arming its DMA descriptor (illustrative). The mailbox adds nothing to \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\), because the seqlock never makes the tick wait for the receive interrupt.
  • Interference \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i =\) 40 μs, the timer and bus interrupts that can preempt the tick within one response window (illustrative).

Math: \[R_i = C_i + B_i + I_i = 250 + 50 + 40 = 340\ \mu\text{s} \le D_i = 400\ \mu\text{s}, \quad D_i - R_i = 60\ \mu\text{s}\]

Systems insight: The margin is only as dependable as its weakest constituent bound. For instance, moving the mailbox copy into a low-priority task guarded by a standard mutex without priority inheritance or priority ceilings leaves \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) completely unbounded, because medium-priority work can preempt the lock holder indefinitely. This is the Mars Pathfinder pathology of war story 4.1. Similarly, permitting unscheduled DMA traffic on the tick’s memory path destroys the upper bound on \(\TaskInterferenceTime_i\). If even a single term loses its bound, the tick lacks a defensible worst-case response time, leaving the stopping budget’s \(\PermissionLoopPeriod\) parameter with no engineering foundation.

↪ How host memory traffic would inflate the permission tick’s interference if both paths shared one die is developed in Contention for Shared Resources.

9 Rate-Monotonic Scheduling: Liu and Layland (1973) established that static-priority rate-monotonic scheduling guarantees periodic task schedulability when total processor utilization satisfies \(\TaskSetUtilization \le \PeriodicTaskCount(2^{1/\PeriodicTaskCount} - 1)\), converging to \(\ln(2) \approx 0.693\) for large \(\PeriodicTaskCount\). The utilization bound is sufficient, not necessary, so a task set that exceeds it may still meet every deadline; with shared-resource blocking and deadlines shorter than periods, only the exact response-time test of Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability decides.

Liu, C. L., and James W. Layland. 1973. “Scheduling Algorithms for Multiprogramming in a Hard-Real-Time Environment.” Journal of the ACM 20 (1): 46–61.
Cervin, Anton, Dan Henriksson, Bo Lincoln, Johan Eker, and Karl-Erik Årzén. 2003. “How Does Control Timing Affect Performance? Analysis and Simulation of Timing Using Jitterbug and TrueTime.” IEEE Control Systems Magazine 23 (3): 16–30. https://doi.org/10.1109/MCS.2003.1200240.

Neither a low average latency nor a modest processor utilization can substitute for that analytical bound,9 because a single missed deadline degrades the machine across multiple architectural layers. When a deadline slips, distributed motor drives must hold their previous position setpoint for an entire additional bus cycle. Consequently, the actuator setpoint lags by a full period (1 ms) rather than the conventional half-period delay associated with a zero-order hold. Repeated deadline misses manifest as timing jitter, which Cervin et al. (2003) analyze as a direct erosion of phase margin in digital feedback loops closed through the tick. Within the safety subsystem, a delayed tick evaluates an expired lease or safety violation late; at the drive velocity limit, each 1 ms delay incurs an irreversible loss of 1.5 mm in physical stopping clearance.

Bounding these response terms requires categorizing every potential source of timing variability according to whether an analytical bound exists:

  1. Bounded Sources: Hardware mechanisms that admit analytical upper bounds from vendor architectural specifications. Examples include deterministic instruction pipelines, round-robin interconnect arbitration, zero-wait-state tightly coupled memory (TCM/SRAM), and deterministic vectored interrupt controllers on real-time microcontrollers.
  2. Experimentally Characterized Sources: Microarchitectural behaviors that cannot be proved strictly from first principles but can be enveloped empirically under frozen hardware configurations. Examples include bounded cache-line misses under hardware cache partitioning, write-buffer draining latencies, periodic DRAM auto-refresh pauses, and thermal throttling profiles stabilized by closed-loop active cooling.
  3. Unbounded Sources: Software and hardware mechanisms that have no finite mathematical worst-case bound and must be purged from the real-time critical path. Examples include operating-system page faults under demand paging, preemptive thread synchronization lacking priority inheritance, dynamic heap allocation over fragmented memory pools, and unscheduled peripheral bus contention.

A credible timing guarantee must therefore explicitly enumerate all permissible operations along the execution path. The hardware partitioning and core isolation strategies from section 4.2 eliminate broad classes of timing interference. A zero-allocation static architecture pre-allocates all buffers and queues at boot time to eliminate memory-management overhead. Neither strategy can eliminate contention for shared memory buses. Response-time analysis must account for every remaining shared-resource transaction, because a high task priority alone never guarantees real-time execution, as the Mars Pathfinder resets showed.

War Story 4.1: The Mars Pathfinder priority inversion (1997)
Context: In July 1997, shortly after landing on Mars, the Mars Pathfinder spacecraft’s avionics computer ran the VxWorks real-time operating system with preemptive priority-based scheduling across mission tasks (Reeves 1997).

Mechanism: A high-priority thread (bc_dist) distributed critical spacecraft bus telemetry, while a low-priority thread (ASI/MET) published meteorological sensor observations through a shared pipe. The mutex serializing access to this pipe was not explicitly declared in mission application code; rather, it was embedded deep within the operating system’s select() implementation to protect internal file-descriptor queues (Reeves 1997). When ASI/MET acquired this internal mutex, long-running medium-priority communications tasks preempted it. Because these medium-priority tasks outranked the meteorological thread, they starved it of CPU cycles, preventing it from releasing the mutex while bc_dist remained blocked, an unbounded priority inversion.

Impact: The blocked bc_dist thread missed its execution deadline. When the supervisory bc_sched watchdog initiated the subsequent bus cycle and detected that bc_dist had not completed, it flagged a critical timing fault and commanded a total system reset. Although telemetry collected prior to the reset survived in non-volatile storage, the resets recurred repeatedly during operational surface maneuvers.

Response: NASA engineers diagnosed the pathology on an identical ground testbed using VxWorks execution tracing, subsequently uploading an inflight software patch that enabled priority inheritance (Sha et al. 1990) on the underlying mutex semaphore.

Systems lesson: Preempting a low-priority lock holder with medium-priority work blocks high-priority consumers indefinitely. Priority inheritance resolves this vulnerability by temporarily elevating the lock holder’s priority to match that of the blocked high-priority task, allowing it to complete its critical section promptly. In robotic actuator and motor-control paths, engineers must either rigorously bound blocking duration (\(\TaskBlockingTime_i\)) using priority inheritance protocols or eliminate shared locks altogether through lock-free communication structures.

4.5.2 Event-triggered and time-triggered bus architectures

Once the permission tick finishes its check on the microcontroller, the admitted setpoint still has to cross a shared physical wire to reach the motor drives, where the same blocking and interference questions reappear. If a high-priority servo command arrives at a shared bus just after a lower-priority diagnostic frame has started transmitting, the command cannot preempt the bits already on the wire and must wait for the frame to finish. Physical buses handle shared medium access in one of two ways. On an event-triggered bus, nodes transmit whenever new data is ready and resolve collisions through dynamic bus arbitration. On a time-triggered bus, a fixed global schedule assigns dedicated transmission windows to each node in advance. Neither design guarantees bounded delivery by its name alone; establishing a worst-case bound requires pricing payload sizes, competing traffic, error-recovery overhead, and device controller delays.

The Controller Area Network (CAN) and its CAN FD variant resolve message collisions via bitwise priority arbitration. While a high-priority frame preempts lower-priority traffic competing for an idle bus, it must wait for any frame already in transmission to finish. Switching to CAN FD accelerates payload serialization by elevating the data-phase bitrate, yet it retains the rule that an in-progress frame cannot be preempted. The blocking duration depends on the longest eligible frame at its nominal and data-phase rates. A valid worst-case response budget must therefore combine both the frame’s own transmission time and the maximum duration of a lower-priority transmission already in progress.

EtherCAT adopts an on-the-fly time-triggered model: an Ethernet coordinator issues a single master frame that traverses all connected motor drives in a physical ring. Dedicated hardware controllers in each drive read incoming setpoints and write sensor feedback directly into designated bit offsets as the Ethernet frame streams through the physical layer. This architecture eliminates arbitration contention across the motor drives, while distributed clock synchronization synchronizes local PWM switching cycles. Time-Sensitive Networking (TSN) achieves comparable determinism over switched Ethernet through IEEE 802.1Qbv time-aware traffic shapers. A TSN delivery guarantee relies on synchronized network gates, bounded clock skew, and strict admission controls that gate best-effort traffic.

↪ The protocol engines and analytical response-time models of concrete fieldbus deployments are developed in Real-Time Fieldbuses and Lock-Free Synchronization.

CAN FD arbitrates a separate frame per message; EtherCAT carries every drive in one frame.

For an integrated mobile manipulator, the core architectural question is whether cyclic actuator commands and feedback fit within the allocated bus period while preserving sufficient margin for MCU control loop execution and motor drive processing. High-bandwidth perception streams (such as camera and LiDAR data) and asynchronous supervisory middleware require separate networks or prioritized channels to prevent unmodeled queuing delays from invading the motor command path. Industrial controllers build that separation into the hardware (figure 5), with ports that serve only the deterministic drive network and local I/O attached through terminals on the controller itself rather than through host software. The running machine does not use this controller; the photograph shows the pattern its own permission path follows.

Clock synchronization aligns the sampling and actuation instants across distributed drives;10 however, phase alignment reveals nothing about the transport latency required for a newly computed setpoint to traverse the physical bus. Even if every drive clock fires simultaneously, the actuators will merely execute stale commands if transit delays stall delivery. A sound design therefore requires independent bounds for both command transport latency and multi-axis synchronization jitter, starting with whether the serialized frames fit inside the cycle at all.

10 Distributed Clock: EtherCAT Distributed Clocks (DC) synchronize distributed drive nodes to a reference clock by measuring propagation delays around the ring. Receive-time latches at each node let the coordinator discipline every node’s timer, so the hardware SYNC0 events that start the drives’ update cycles occur in close alignment. Clock alignment alone does not bound the complete control cycle, which must also account for controller response time and PWM gate update latency.

4.5.3 Comparing bus occupancy on CAN FD and EtherCAT

If all nine servo axes of the mobile manipulator share a single CAN FD bus, arbitration headers and worst-case bit stuffing push the command and feedback frames alone past the 1 ms bus cycle, before any controller delay or bus contention is counted. To keep each link within its cycle budget, communication links on the mobile manipulator separate into three tiers by rate and scope:

  1. Board-Level Peripheral Interconnects (SPI, Memory-Mapped I/O): Synchronous SPI links and memory-mapped register transfers link local microcontrollers directly to joint position encoders, current-shunt analog-to-digital converters (ADCs), and inverter gate drivers with sub-microsecond latency (\(< 1\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)) along rigid, hardware-timed schedules.
  2. Deterministic Drive Command Buses (EtherCAT, TSN Ethernet): Deterministic industrial networks transport cyclic joint position setpoints and motor feedback across all distributed drive inverters within an allocated 1 ms bus cycle (\(\CommandTransportTime\)), maintaining sub-microsecond synchronization jitter (\(\TimingJitter < 100\text{ ns}\)).
  3. Chassis and Auxiliary Diagnostic Buses (CAN FD): Priority-arbitrated buses service lower-rate peripherals, such as battery management systems (BMS), thermal monitors, and chassis diagnostics, where arbitration delays remain acceptable under verified worst-case utilization bounds.

None of these bounds belongs to a protocol acronym alone. Each guarantee depends on the physical network topology, transceiver hardware, payload geometries, and cyclic schedules. The first check is whether every servo axis can exchange its command and feedback payloads within the permission path’s allocated bus cycle budget.

Napkin Math 4.3: Fieldbus serialization before schedulability
Problem: The mobile manipulator’s permission path exchanges position setpoints and feedback with its servo axes once per bus cycle. How much of that cycle do CAN FD frames, or one EtherCAT frame, occupy before controller and device delays are counted, and which buses pass this necessary check?

Variables:

  • 9 servo axes, 7 arm joints and 2 drive wheels (datasheet), exchanged every 1 ms bus cycle (illustrative).
  • An 8-byte record per axis in each direction, a 32-bit position and two 16-bit words (chosen).
  • CAN FD at \(1\text{ Mbps}\) nominal and \(5\text{ Mbps}\) in the data phase (chosen). A CAN frame carries one message, so each axis needs a command frame and a feedback frame. The arbitration field and the trailer run at the nominal rate, 34 bits; the data-length code, payload, stuff count, and 17-bit CRC run at the data rate, 113 bits (International Organization for Standardization 2015; Robert Bosch GmbH 2012). Both counts include the worst-case 21 dynamic stuff bits, because a bound must assume the bit pattern that lengthens the frame most.
  • EtherCAT at \(100\text{ Mbps}\) (datasheet), one frame carrying every axis’s command and feedback with a 64-byte header, and a forwarding delay of 0.6 μs per node, the shift-register pass-through of a slave controller that never buffers the frame (both illustrative).

Math: One CAN FD frame, then all frames on one bus and on an arm bus: \[t_{\text{frame}} = \frac{34\ \text{bits}}{1\ \text{Mbps}} + \frac{113\ \text{bits}}{5\ \text{Mbps}} = 34 + 22.6 = 56.6\ \mu\text{s}\] \[18 \times 56.6 = 1018.8\ \mu\text{s} > 1000\ \mu\text{s}, \qquad 14 \times 56.6 = 792.4\ \mu\text{s}\] The base’s 4 frames take 226.4 μs. For EtherCAT, serialization plus forwarding: \[\frac{(64 + 9 \times 16)\ \text{B} \times 8}{100\ \text{Mbps}} + 9 \times 0.6\ \mu\text{s} = 16.64 + 5.4 = 22.04\ \mu\text{s}\]

Result: One CAN FD bus fills 101.9 percent of the cycle on serialization alone. A bus per limb passes, the arm bus at 79.2 percent with 207.6 μs left for blocking, competing frames, and retransmissions. EtherCAT occupies 2.2 percent and leaves 978.0 μs unallocated by this calculation.

Systems insight: CAN FD’s accelerated data phase cannot salvage a single shared bus topology. Every transmitted frame still incurs arbitration and trailer bit times at the lower nominal rate, and CAN requires distinct command and feedback frames for each joint axis. EtherCAT, in contrast, aggregates all axes into a single circulating telegram processed on the fly. Neither occupancy figure represents an end-to-end response bound. A rigorous CAN FD analysis must incorporate message arbitration, non-preemptive blocking, and retransmissions, whereas an EtherCAT budget must include coordinator cycle jitter, slave hardware propagation, and drive firmware latencies. Any unmodeled delay that creeps into the physical command path consumes stopping clearance at a rate of \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\UnmodeledCommandPathDelay\).

Robert Bosch GmbH. 2012. CAN with Flexible Data-Rate: Specification Version 1.0. Robert Bosch GmbH.

4.5.4 Bandwidth, response time, and multi-axis synchronization jitter

A network link with plenty of raw bandwidth can still deliver a joint setpoint late if frames queue behind camera traffic. A bus that delivers setpoints quickly on average can still twist a coordinated multi-joint motion if the drives apply their updates at slightly different instants. Analyzing serialization occupancy separates three metrics that industrial datasheets often conflate: raw bandwidth, command response time, and multi-axis synchronization jitter. Bandwidth governs the total payload volume the link can carry per unit time. Command response time measures the complete latency, including queueing, arbitration, transmission, and drive input decoding, before an actuator applies a setpoint. Synchronization jitter bounds the time spread across multi-axis updates. A network design can improve any one of these metrics while leaving the others unconstrained.

Set against the cycle, the notebook’s occupancies separate into one failure and three passes (figure 6). Only the single shared CAN FD bus fails, needing 1018.8 μs before any contention or retransmission is counted. The per-limb buses (792.4 μs for the arm) and EtherCAT (22.0 μs) leave room for controller execution and clock synchronization. Staying within the cycle budget provides neither a synchronization-jitter bound nor a complete response bound. A defensible CAN FD analysis requires upper bounds on asynchronous message interference and non-preemptive blocking. An EtherCAT path demands explicit master cycle jitter, slave hardware propagation, and drive firmware latencies. A TSN schedule requires reserved transmission windows and bounded clock-synchronization errors.11 These factors are required inputs to the response-time equation, not inherent guarantees achieved by selecting a protocol standard.

11 Time-Sensitive Networking: IEEE 802.1Qbv Time-Sensitive Networking provides bounded Ethernet delivery by configuring time-aware traffic shapers across network switches. High-priority control frames travel inside dedicated, pre-scheduled transmission windows, while asynchronous high-bandwidth traffic (such as uncompressed camera streams) is held back at the switch gates. This scheduled time-division multiplexing lets camera streams and microsecond servo commands share a physical link without packet collisions or switch-queue buildup.

Actuator commands also demand strict temporal freshness. Even when delivered comfortably within a deterministic transport window, a setpoint computed from stale perception is functionally hazardous. The evidence timestamp and lease expiry embedded in the command proposal remain safeguards even over nanosecond-synchronized fieldbuses. The transport budget must reserve adequate headroom for physical actuation to execute, and the real-time permission path must reject any setpoint whose authoritative validity window has expired.

Checkpoint 4.1: Response bounds and command transport

The lease budget counts the permission tick and the bus cycle as bounded terms. These items test whether you can supply the bounds it assumes.

A fieldbus path qualified under declared traffic and fault conditions bounds command delivery within its allocated cycle, but on-time delivery says nothing about whether the command should have been sent. That depends on which node may issue setpoints at all, and on whether each setpoint is checked against the machine’s physical limits before it leaves.

4.6 Actuation Authority

A shared mailbox cannot protect a mobile manipulator if the host processor retains a diagnostic route, an auxiliary bus master, or a JTAG debug port that writes directly to a drive. The silicon partition established in section 4.2 therefore requires exclusive physical control over every electrical path capable of energizing the motor windings. Actuation authority is this exclusive physical ability to modify drive states, encompassing PWM configuration registers, current references, gate-driver outputs, and bridge-enable lines. Under this architecture, the host publishes candidate proposals \(\CandidateProposal_t\) and the safety microcontroller’s permission path alone turns them into admitted commands \(\AdmittedCommand_t\). The difference between holding that authority and merely appearing to is a question of wiring (figure 7). When the permission path is the sole writer of the drive registers and can trip a drive inhibit, every motion passes its checks. When the host can also write those registers, one unchecked write reaches the motors, and proposer and drives share a single failure domain.

↪ How exclusive authority over each actuator channel passes between the learned policy, an operator, and the fallback is developed in Authority states and single-writer actuator ownership.

12 Hardware Trip Zone: Microcontroller hardware trip zones couple analog overcurrent and overvoltage comparators directly to timer output logic. Upon fault detection, configured trip logic may force gate signals high-impedance or activate a validated braking mode. These have different mechanical consequences: torque removal can leave the plant coasting, while dynamic braking requires a rated energy path.

The microcontroller continuously evaluates physical safety invariants: verifying that commanded joint positions lie within certified reach envelopes, that joint velocities stay within qualified limits, and that projected decelerations remain safely within dynamic stopping clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)). When a proposal satisfies all invariants, the microcontroller admits the command and interpolates waypoints into cyclic position setpoints for each bus cycle, which the drives track through their internal velocity and current loops. When a candidate proposal violates an invariant or expires, the enforcer refuses it (\(\bot\)). A refusal does not automatically revoke a previously admitted, actively executing trajectory chunk. Instead, the active chunk continues running as long as its state, sensor evidence, and chunk lease remain valid. When those conditions lapse or fail, the enforcer terminates execution authority and transitions the system into a controlled deceleration or hold. In parallel, a separate hardware trip zone stands ready to cut motor drive torque upon catastrophic electrical faults, though de-energizing an inverter does not by itself brake moving mass.12

↪ The refusal authority and the state invariants it checks are developed in Refusing Proposals and Enforcing State Invariants.

At the causal boundary the root cause of an aberrant proposal does not matter. A remote memory exploit in a host operating system daemon, an adversarial optical perturbation that induces a vision model hallucination, and an arithmetic overflow in an acceleration tensor engine can all produce invalid proposals for the same boundary filter to reject. A proposal that passes the physical checks may still pursue the wrong task, so admission does not establish semantic correctness. Because the enforcer evaluates physical invariant bounds against direct sensor feedback from optical encoders and current shunts rather than inspecting internal neural network weights, it requires no internal model of why the proposer failed. Because the host lacks bus-master privileges to actuator registers, a compromised host has no bypass around this check. Complete protection against a rogue host nonetheless requires four physical foundations: independent sensing, an accurate kinematic and dynamic limit model, sufficient actuator capability, and a physically feasible fallback trajectory.

↩︎ The causal boundary, where a command becomes current in a winding and mass begins to move, is established in From a proposed action to motion.

Controlled stopping and torque removal impose different engineering obligations. Powered braking actively uses the drive inverters to decelerate the plant to zero velocity before disabling torque. Immediate torque removal, by contrast, de-energizes the motor windings instantly, which can leave a wheeled base coasting uncontrollably or allow a vertically loaded manipulator arm to collapse under gravity. Maintaining a held position requires either continuous motor torque or a certified mechanical restraint. At the actuation authority interface, the systems designer must explicitly designate the physical fallback response and reserve both the execution time and thermal energy budget that it demands.

A refusal that leaves no admissible active continuation carries an unavoidable physical cost. When the enforcer rejects an invalid proposal and commands dynamic braking on a fast-moving joint, the physical braking path must supply sufficient torque, clearance, and thermal dissipation capacity to arrest the moving mass. Over an arrest interval spanning several hundred milliseconds, the motor winding resistance, dynamic brake chopper resistor, or friction brake lining must absorb the joint’s accumulated kinetic energy within the thermal limits detailed in Thermal Duty Cycles. This dissipation must occur without degrading winding insulation or permitting the manipulator to breach its physical clearance boundary.

↪ The stopping-distance model this braking path must satisfy is developed in Kinematic Stopping Envelopes and Information Age Lag.

13 Safe Torque Off (STO): An STO implementation removes torque-producing drive signals through a hardware interlock. An IEC 61800-5-2 integrity rating depends on the actual devices, diagnostic coverage, wiring, and validation, not on the circuit pattern alone (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016).

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016. IEC 61800-5-2:2016: Adjustable Speed Electrical Power Drive Systems, Part 5-2: Safety Requirements, Functional. International Electrotechnical Commission.

If the safety microcontroller itself hangs or suffers a clock failure, it cannot execute a software-directed stop. A hardwired, out-of-band interlock must therefore supervise the system and enforce a qualified fallback state. Safe Torque Off (STO) accomplishes this by disabling gate-driver optocouplers or charge pumps through hardware logic, preventing the inverter from generating rotating magnetic fields.13 STO does not isolate the high-voltage DC bus from the mains, nor does it guarantee that momentum will dissipate within the stopping clearance allocated for powered deceleration. Complete electrical isolation requires an upstream safety contactor, and containing gravitational drop or mechanical drift requires a certified electromechanical brake. A dual-channel interlock circuit (the relay network of figure 8) decouples torque inhibition entirely from the host software stack. Safety qualification must still verify that the system remains resilient against common-cause failures in power rails, clock crystals, and harness wiring.

In figure 8, dual positive-break E-stop contacts (\(\EmergencyStopContact_1\), \(\EmergencyStopContact_2\)) drive independent force-guided relays (\(\ForceGuidedRelay_1\), \(\ForceGuidedRelay_2\)), which route dual STO enable signals into the motor drive. Series-wired normally closed external device monitoring (EDM) contacts prevent system restart if either relay fails to open. At the bottom, a dedicated brake supply drops a spring-applied mechanical brake directly onto the motor shaft upon power loss, enforcing torque removal (\(\MotorTorque = 0\)) and holding restraint without software or processor intervention. However, engineers must not confuse a static holding brake with a dynamic stopping brake. Under normal operational sequences, the drive decelerates the motor to zero velocity electrically before engaging the spring brake to hold position. Dropping a holding brake onto a spinning shaft during a high-speed emergency stop subjects the brake pads and hub to severe friction heating and mechanical shock, requiring independent thermal rating and stopping-distance validation. The stop-category selections and timing sequences depend directly on these electromechanical limits.

Systems Perspective 4.1: Hardware authority invariant
If an unverified learned policy has direct memory-mapped write access to actuator gate-driver registers, the machine has no mediated proposal path, and its next out-of-distribution input reaches the motors unchecked.

Write privilege is a physical reality dictated by memory mapping, bus arbitration, and physical trace layout, never a configurable software flag. The host processor holds no write privilege to drive registers. While hardware partitioning determines which processor may issue physical commands to an actuator, the engineer must still decide what physical quantity those admitted commands specify, because each choice leaves a different set of failure modes for the permission path to catch.

4.6.1 What a proposal may command

A digital motor drive accepts setpoints across a nested cascade of control loops: position, velocity, and winding current, with current running innermost at the highest bandwidth. Sending raw current targets directly to the drive bypasses its position and velocity loops and leaves the permission path to predict how gravity, inertia, and external contact will move the joint. Sending pure position targets lets the drive bound its own speed and current, at the cost of ramping winding current against an unmodeled obstacle until the inverter saturates or the mechanism breaks. The level at which a proposal enters this cascade therefore decides which physical states the drive hardware regulates on its own and which quantities the permission path must verify before admitting the command. Four command interfaces dominate physical AI systems, each placing a different prediction and checking burden on the enforcer.

A position target \(\JointPositionTarget\) delegates the entire trajectory tracking problem to the drive’s internal cascaded loops. Because the drive firmware enforces its own configured speed and current limits, the enforcer’s verification burden remains relatively compact. It validates that the target lies within the geometric reach envelope, bounds the discrete step between consecutive targets (which position gains convert directly into velocity and torque demands), and ensures adequate dynamic stopping clearance. However, a pure position target cannot modulate interaction force. When encountering an unmodeled rigid obstacle, a stiff proportional-integral position loop interprets physical contact as a tracking error to be eliminated, ramping winding current until the inverter saturates or the mechanical structure breaks. The impedance-control analysis in Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture quantified this exact failure mode on a cage-door latch.

A velocity target \(\JointVelocityTarget\) shifts kinematic integration to the host proposer, bypassing the drive’s position loop. Because the proposal omits an explicit geometric endpoint, the enforcer cannot simply validate a target coordinate against static workspace limits. Instead, the enforcer forward-integrates the commanded velocity over the duration of the active chunk lease to predict the joint’s worst-case state at lease expiration. It then checks both the reach envelope and the dynamic stopping distance against this forward projection. As with pure position control, contact force remains unregulated, and unmodeled collisions will produce maximum drive torque.

A torque target \(\JointTorqueTarget\), delivered as a direct current reference, bypasses both outer loops. The drive regulates only electromagnetic winding current. Consequently, all kinematic state evolution, including velocity, position, and braking distance, becomes an emergent consequence of plant dynamics, gravity, and external contact forces rather than an enforced setpoint. The enforcer must bound the resulting state evolution and preserve a feasible fallback, using a plant model or effort-space constraints, while constraining torque slew rates and tracking the cumulative mechanical energy injected into the system. Torque control offers maximum responsiveness for dynamic locomotion and manipulation, but it imposes the heaviest computational burden on the permission path while providing the least explicit intent.

An impedance target specifies a virtual equilibrium pose alongside stiffness gains \(\PidProportionalGain\) and damping gains \(\PidDerivativeGain\), so the actuator behaves as a virtual spring-damper (Hogan 1985), the same model introduced in Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture: \[\ImpedanceRestoringForce = \PidProportionalGain (\ImpedanceEquilibriumPosition - \ImpedanceControlledPosition) + \PidDerivativeGain (\ImpedanceEquilibriumVelocity - \ImpedanceControlledVelocity)\] Its dynamic dual, an admittance target, measures external contact force via load cells and commands motion in response. Both formulations explicitly regulate the dynamic relationship between motion and force, a coupling that position, velocity, and torque interfaces leave unconstrained. This capability makes impedance and admittance formulations standard in contact-rich manipulation tasks. The enforcer’s verification role expands accordingly: it must evaluate both the equilibrium setpoint and the commanded stiffness gains. Under quasi-static contact, the maximum contact force is bounded by the product of the commanded stiffness and the largest displacement the environment can impose. Consequently, a stiffness parameter that exceeds the structural limits of the workpiece or fixture is just as inadmissible as a trajectory crossing a workspace wall. However, steady-state gains do not govern transient impact forces, which depend heavily on approach velocity, rotor inertia, structural compliance, and control-loop latency. Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers establishes the energy and contact-force barriers needed to bound these transient impact phases.

Hogan, Neville. 1985. “Impedance Control: An Approach to Manipulation: Part i–Theory, Part II–Implementation, Part III–Applications.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 107 (1): 1–24.

On the reference mobile manipulator, the enforcer admits position waypoints, interpolating them into fine setpoints for each bus cycle while the motor drives close their own internal velocity and current loops. When the manipulator engages the sprung latch of an equipment cage door, the host switches to an impedance command, introducing stiffness and damping parameters that the real-time tick must validate before transmission. No command interface eliminates the verification burden of the permission path: shifting the command interface inward, from position setpoints toward raw current references, strips away explicit geometric intent and forces the safety layer to predict complex electrodynamic behavior. Write privilege and command filtering protect the physical plant only if the enforcer also maintains physical independence from the host whose proposals it evaluates. If the safety microcontroller and the host application processor share a power rail, a common system clock, or a unified memory bus, a failure in the host can brown out the microcontroller, corrupt its timing, or deadlock the bus. Any of these silences the safety layer without issuing a single illegal drive command.

4.7 Hardware Isolation and Fault Containment

A large current transient during a neural accelerator burst can pull down a shared power rail and brown out or reset the permission processor without warning, while memory bus contention on a shared interconnect can delay safety checks past the hard real-time execution tick. Even when safety enforcement executes on a dedicated processor, physical coupling to the proposal engine can silently undermine software isolation. Containing faults across the two paths therefore requires isolating or bounding shared physical resources across four domains: execution hardware, reference oscillators, power distribution rails, and memory hierarchies.14 Where any of those resources remain shared, the architecture must demonstrate that cross-domain interference and declared common-cause faults cannot prevent the permission path from meeting its execution deadline or initiating its qualified fallback.

14 Freedom from Interference (FFI): ISO 26262 defines FFI in its vocabulary, Part 1, as the absence of cascading failures between two or more elements that could lead to the violation of a safety requirement (International Organization for Standardization 2018). Part 6 lists the software interference it covers (timing and execution, memory, and exchange of information), whereas shared power, clocks, and heat are common-cause and cascading faults that Part 9 treats in its dependent-failure analysis and that IEC 61508 addresses as independence between elements (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010b). A neural policy sharing silicon with the permission path therefore needs both arguments, FFI for its software and a dependent-failure analysis for the resources underneath.

International Organization for Standardization. 2018. ISO 26262:2018: Road Vehicles, Functional Safety. International Organization for Standardization.
International Electrotechnical Commission. 2010b. IEC 61508-2:2010: Functional Safety of Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems, Part 2: Requirements for Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems. International Electrotechnical Commission.

The running machine’s two-processor architecture addresses each of these four fault paths with an explicit hardware design choice. The permission path executes from private memory over a dedicated board-level link, clocks from an independent oscillator, and draws power from the isolated rail specified in Cross-budget coupling and permission rail isolation rather than from the primary 47.7 V drive bus feeding the application processor. Diode-isolated storage capacitors hold up this safety rail for 2 s. If a transient load fault pulls the drive bus down to the host converter’s undervoltage lockout threshold at 33 V, the safety microcontroller stays powered long enough to capture the undervoltage interrupt, log the event, and command a graceful fallback while the primary host resets. This holds only if the required detector, actuator power, and braking path survive the fault.

Keeping the logic powered does not guarantee motor torque or braking capability. Bringing a moving machine to a controlled stop requires sufficient DC bus energy, healthy inverter stages, and adequate obstacle clearance. If electrical drive power collapses entirely, the system must execute a qualified unpowered fallback whose mechanical brake engagement delay and subsequent stopping motion fit within the pre-allocated physical envelope. These isolation guarantees are design assertions that must be empirically verified on physical hardware. Every shared dependency must be carried forward into the formal verification handoff record.

↪ The four substrate couplings that board layout must sever, and the fault-injection experiments that test their isolation, are developed in Two Paths and the Resources They Share.

A sag on the shared bus reaches every load; the permission rail rides through on its own.

Substrate isolation ensures that the permission path stays alive when the host crashes, but the enforcer’s downstream action depends entirely on how the proposer failed: through unresponsiveness or through corruption. Unresponsiveness occurs when an inference pipeline deadlocks, thread scheduling misses a deadline, or kernel memory fragmentation stalls process execution, yielding no new trajectory packets. A complete host crash trips the hardware heartbeat timer. Conversely, the host operating system might remain healthy while the user-space neural policy pipeline hangs; in that scenario, the proposer stops emitting admissible action chunks, allowing the active chunk’s lease or evidence deadline to lapse. These complementary deadlines form the supervisory timing hierarchy established in section 4.4.1.

Corruption occurs when the proposer continues running and satisfies its heartbeat deadline, but outputs invalid or hazardous setpoints. Examples include kinematically unreachable joint configurations, step discontinuities in velocity requests, or floating-point NaNs and infinities caused by out-of-distribution visual tokens or matrix overflow. The enforcer handles these two failure profiles through orthogonal mechanisms: unresponsiveness triggers trajectory revocation as soon as the chunk lease or the heartbeat timer expires, whereas corruption prompts an immediate invariant rejection at the boundary filter upon the arrival of the malformed packet. A live heartbeat therefore never extends motion authority; only an admitted chunk renews it, and authority ends when the chunk lease runs out. Whether the chunk policy can renew quickly enough to keep the machine moving, and why the slower intent model supplies goals rather than holding chunk leases itself, is the coupled budget of section 4.9.1.

When active motion authority lapses without an admissible continuation, the permission path must execute an explicit degradation strategy rather than cutting motor bus power unconditionally. As detailed in Unpowered states and unmanaged fallback dynamics, de-energizing an inverter drops the actuators into uncontrolled freewheeling or dynamic short-circuit braking, neither of which produces an orderly, predictable stop. Instead, the safety path evaluates the plant’s instantaneous kinematic state and selects the safest feasible deceleration profile. An optional brief continuation along the last validated trajectory is permissible only while its state estimates, sensor evidence validity, chunk lease window, and obstacle clearance margins all remain valid.

Once that lease expires, any separately authorized fallback delay \(\FallbackHold\) is absorbed into the total reaction latency \(\PrebrakeDelay\). It must fit, together with the evidence age, within the 176.3 ms left under the pre-brake ceiling once the lease path and brake onset are charged at the drive limit (notebook 4.1). If measured latency bounds exceed this headroom, the enforcer must initiate deceleration sooner. Controlled braking uses the available actuator torque characterized in Actuator Transmission Limits to arrest motion within the remaining clearance. On gravity-loaded manipulators, motion arrest must be backed by engaging rated mechanical holding brakes once the joints reach standstill, preventing gravitational backdriving when drive power is subsequently removed.

After the physical plant comes to a complete halt, the architecture executes a recovery handshake before restoring autonomous proposal authority. The safety microcontroller asserts a persistent FAULT_HELD latch in its exported status register and publishes the plant’s confirmed physical rest pose \((\RestConfiguration, \GeneralizedVelocities = \ZeroVector)\). In turn, the application processor must discard all pending action chunks, invalidate internal state tied to the interrupted motion, capture fresh sensor observations tagged with synchronized hardware timestamps, and formulate a newly aligned trajectory proposal.

This recovery sequence must also purge any proposal-side state that assumes the robot executed its planned trajectory to completion. For autoregressive policies and VLA models, this requires flushing token buffers and the key-value (KV) attention cache; retaining pre-fault keys and values would bias future action generation toward a physical trajectory that was interrupted. However, clearing software caches does not by itself establish physical continuity. Before deasserting the FAULT_HELD latch and restoring proposal authority, the safety microcontroller inspects the opening waypoint \(\ProposalFirstWaypoint\) of the incoming proposal, verifying that it aligns with the robot’s measured physical rest pose within a strict spatial bound (\(||\ProposalFirstWaypoint - \RestConfiguration|| \le \RecoveryPoseTolerance\)).

Architectural independence cannot simply be read off a schematic or block diagram; it must be empirically validated against identified common-cause faults on the physical board. Similarly, the chunk lease that constrains a malfunctioning proposer derives directly from physical dynamics, sized from the stopping margin that remains after detection delays and actuator responses. Together, these defenses bound a proposer that crashes, stalls, or publishes corrupted actions. The separation leaves one question open, whether a neural policy engineered to execute deterministically at the enforcer’s own update rate still requires an external runtime monitor.

↪ The fault injection that tests this independence on the physical board is developed in Fault Injection on Hardware.

4.8 Invariant Checking

A proposer fast enough to keep pace with the permission tick invites a shortcut. Learned locomotion controllers already emit joint targets within an order of magnitude of a millisecond, and a designer may be tempted to let such a policy write the motor registers directly. Rate does not confer authority. A fast network emitting joint targets or torques carries the same statistical uncertainty as a large model generating waypoints a few times a second. Even when policy and enforcer share one period, the network’s output remains a proposal that must pass the proposal boundary before it changes current in a winding. What the permission path can still guarantee when a learned policy commands at control rate, and what it gives up, is examined in Where the thesis bends.

The interface must reject an inadmissible candidate without waiting for the producer, and recheck the active command against current physical state. Position and velocity limits (\(\GeneralizedCoordinates \in [\JointPositionLowerLimit, \JointPositionUpperLimit]\) and \(|\GeneralizedVelocities| \le \JointVelocityLimit\)) are necessary but do not by themselves establish a feasible stop. A torque-slew constraint, \(|\JointTorqueSlew| = |(\JointTorque_k - \JointTorque_{k-1})/\FiniteChange \TimeInstant| \le \JointTorqueSlewLimit\), can limit command changes, but a command that meets the slew limit must still satisfy the other physical limits. Contact may additionally require accounting for exchanged energy, \(\int \JointTorque^\top \GeneralizedVelocities\,d\TimeInstant\).

↪ The passivity framework that accounts for energy exchanged in contact is developed in Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction.

Choosing and composing those filters is the work of Safety Enforcement, which constructs the admissibility calculation and selects a feasible continuation, hold, or stop. What the Nervous System fixes is the execution contract those filters must run under, whatever they compute. On the running machine the contract has numbers. The recheck of the active command must finish inside the tick’s 400 μs deadline under the response bound of equation 3. It must read measured wheel and joint encoder state rather than commanded setpoints (the fallacy of section 4.10 shows what the commanded setpoints miss). It must also leave the safety microcontroller the only writer of the drive registers. Neither fast inference nor a fast rejection routine establishes that contract on its own.

The same reasoning covers the one proposer that is not learned. A remote operator is a third proposer. Its commands pass the same permission path, and network delay spends the same pre-brake budget. Neither speed nor a human origin gives a proposal authority; only passing the permission path’s checks does, and each check spends part of a budget that the next section lays out handoff by handoff.

↪ How an operator’s reaction and link delays are charged against the stopping budget is developed in Human reaction time and travel before braking.

Checkpoint 4.2: Hardware isolation and invariant checking

Before turning to the handoffs between proposer and actuator, verify your understanding of the exchange, hardware isolation, and pre-actuation invariant defenses:

4.9 The Handoffs Matrix

When a mobile manipulator overruns a stop, the diagnosis begins by locating the fault at one of five canonical handoffs between the photons collected by its cameras and the current delivered to its drives. A cloud service governs its interfaces with statistical service-level objectives (SLOs), such as a \(\text{p99}\) request latency; when a tail event violates that target, the runtime absorbs the delay through retries, request rerouting, or graceful degradation. The five handoffs of an embodied machine define hard cyber-physical service-level objectives instead. Software cannot recall physical momentum or undo resistive heating in an actuator winding, so no retry mechanism can mask an unbudgeted interface violation.

Upstream, sensing hardware transfers raw transducer signals to perception pipelines. Perception yields intermediate geometric and semantic representations that fuse into an estimated world state. This state feeds the learned policy to produce an action plan, which in turn submits candidate trajectories to the safety enforcer. Finally, the approved command passes from the enforcer to the physical motor drives. Across each boundary, five interface limits govern feasibility: latency (\(\Latency\)), bandwidth (\(\Bandwidth\)), energy and heat (\(\HeatDissipationPower\)), determinism (\(\TimingJitter\)), and fault domain isolation (\(\FaultDomainIsolation\)). The five handoffs and five interface limits form a five-by-five matrix mapping every signal path to the physical budgets of the machine. These interface limits are distinct from the body’s five mechanical budgets, which enter the matrix primarily through its latency and energy columns. The contract that records these budgets, the handoff record defined below, joins the physical limit records of The six elements of a limit record to the proposer limits of the Brain. Latency, renewal rate, and evidence age are therefore written in one schema.

↩︎ The body’s five mechanical budgets are established in The Five Physical Budgets.

The allocation in table 3 illustrates which limits are design-limiting at each handoff, using chosen design budgets rather than empirical measurements. The matrix gathers the physical budgets of the machine’s mechanical, cognitive, and sensory systems, together with the sensor capture that the five-level machine model places at the Boundary. In the Boundary row (\(\SignalHandoff_1\)), where the physical world becomes digital measurement, bandwidth and latency bind first: raw multi-camera pixel streams saturate the serializer-deserializer (SerDes) lanes, and the exposure window imposes a strict floor on image age before serialization even begins. The Brain rows (\(\SignalHandoff_2\), \(\SignalHandoff_3\)) bind first on energy and latency, reflecting the heavy inference costs of the learned proposers. The Body row (\(\SignalHandoff_5\)) binds first on determinism and heat, because the drives’ current loops must hold their timing jitter below \(10\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), and every ampere delivered dissipates heat across the windings and power inverter stages.

The Nervous System row (\(\SignalHandoff_4\)) forms the architectural bridge coupling the Brain and the Body. The proposal boundary between the learned planner and the enforcer carries an action proposal (\(\CandidateProposal_t\)), typically structured as a compact spline parameterization or waypoint chunk. For the compact payload assumed in this grid, the required throughput is modest (\(\Bandwidth < 100\text{ kB/s}\)), so bandwidth does not bind; instead, latency, determinism (\(\TimingJitter\)), and fault domain isolation (\(\FaultDomainIsolation\)) govern the interface. The permission path isolates stochastic model proposals using nonblocking mailboxes and expiring leases. On the mobile manipulator, the latency limit is the chunk lease, which consumes 60 ms of the 258.3 ms pre-brake ceiling available at the drive limit (section 4.4), while the active command is rechecked within each 1 ms permission tick. Full trajectory admission has a separate computation and response budget and must complete before any candidate chunk activates; the tick interval does not imply that arbitrary occupancy queries or whole-trajectory collision checks fit within that brief slice. Fault isolation relies on a private peripheral bus, an independent oscillator, and the isolated, held-up power rail detailed in section 4.7.

From the causal boundary perspective, any interface failure localizes to the specific handoff and budget it violates. Semantic mistakes or flawed physical assumptions can still escape an otherwise timely interface; the matrix does not substitute for task-level evaluation or hazard analysis. When diagnosing an over-budget stop, trajectory overrun, or subsystem fault, engineers navigate table 3 through a three-step operational root-cause procedure:

  1. Locate the boundary seam: Identify which physical handoff (\(\SignalHandoff_1\text{--}\SignalHandoff_5\)) recorded the initial timing excursion, sequence gap, torn payload, or thermal trip.
  2. Check the binding dimension: Query the specific physical limit (\(\Latency, \Bandwidth, \HeatDissipationPower, \TimingJitter, \FaultDomainIsolation\)) designated as BINDING for that handoff cell, identifying the primary resource bottleneck governing the interface.
  3. Evaluate like-for-like evidence: Compare runtime diagnostic traces directly against the recorded bound kind (requirement, proven bound, or measured distribution), verifying whether the failure stemmed from an unmodeled microarchitectural tail event or a direct physical budget violation.

A handoff diagnosis must strictly compare like evidence. For the \(\SignalHandoff_2\) state-estimation path, suppose engineers allocate a \(30.0\text{ ms}\) latency budget and measure \(\text{p99} = 24.5\text{ ms}\) during a baseline hardware-in-the-loop run, yielding a sampled margin of \(+5.5\text{ ms}\). Under thermal throttling, a subsequent run measuring \(\text{p99} = 85.0\text{ ms}\) reveals a \(-55.0\text{ ms}\) deficit against that design budget. The appropriate remedy is to reduce or reallocate computational workload while preserving independent safety enforcement during revalidation. A sampled \(\text{p99}\) latency diagnoses a performance regression; it does not constitute a worst-case timing proof.

Definition 4.3: Handoff record

Handoff record is the record that states one interface limit at one of the five handoffs between sensing and actuation: its value, the conditions under which it holds, its uncertainty and margin, the detector that catches an excursion, its provenance, and whether the value is a requirement, a proven bound, or a measured distribution.

  1. Significance: It is the second record in the record chain, after the limit record of the Body, and the first that ties a timing claim to the interface where the time is spent. On the running machine its \(\SignalHandoff_4\) entry is the 60 ms chunk lease, recorded as a requirement whose margin depends on the intervals of table 1.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a limit record, which describes a physical envelope of the plant such as credible deceleration, a handoff record describes an interface between two subsystems and names the clock on which its times are expressed. Unlike a latency in a profiling log, it states its bound kind, so a percentile cannot pass for a worst case.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording a bench \(\text{p99}\) as a proven bound. The record then carries a number that the permission path trusts in conditions the bench never reached.

Every boundary condition in the grid is recorded this way, so the budgets survive changes of hardware, firmware, and model. Each entry carries the six elements of a limit record and adds the fields that locate the limit at a handoff and state what evidence supports it (table 4).

The four timing events of table 1 sit on this grid’s handoffs, capture within \(\SignalHandoff_1\), dispatch at \(\SignalHandoff_2\), admission at \(\SignalHandoff_4\), and brake onset at \(\SignalHandoff_5\). The grid’s latency column therefore follows the same single execution path as the timing table, and every time in it is expressed on the safety microcontroller’s clock. Together, those rows show that the chunk lease at \(\SignalHandoff_4\) cannot be chosen in isolation from the stopping distance below it or the inference renewal rate above it.

4.9.1 Coupling the distance, lease, and supply budgets

Shortening the chunk lease to save stopping distance, without checking how fast the proposer renews, causes the lease to expire before the next chunk arrives, so the robot stutters from stop to stop. Lengthening the lease to ride out slow renewals lets the robot overrun its rack-end clearance before braking begins. The machine’s physical, cognitive, and nervous subsystems therefore meet at a single shared budget, with the chunk lease at their point of convergence. In Kinetic Momentum, the loaded machine moving at its 1.5 m/s drive limit had to come to a complete stop within the 1.10 m clearance available at a rack end. Accounting for deceleration mechanics and fixed braking overheads left 258.3 ms for all computational and communication activities preceding brake onset. Section 4.4 then allocated a portion of this window: when a proposer stalls, it retains motion authority for one chunk lease before the emergency stop command reaches the motor drives one tick and one bus cycle later, bringing the running stopping budget to 835.5 mm.

Because the proposer needs enough time to renew that lease before it expires, The Cost of a Fresh Evaluation sets the lower bound from what each learned model can supply. The intent model’s INT4 weights require 24.5 ms just to stream across the memory bus. A complete inference pass of compute-bound prefill followed by bandwidth-bound autoregressive decoding consumes 336 ms, so at 2 Hz the intent model cannot renew proposals on tens-of-milliseconds timescales. By contrast, the action chunking policy streams its parameters in 1.12 ms, and its target 20 Hz cadence supplies a fresh trajectory chunk every 50 ms. Its illustrative 40 ms \(\text{p99}\) inference latency makes this cadence a feasible nominal supply target rather than a guaranteed worst-case bound, so intercepting late, dropped, or corrupt renewals remains the responsibility of the independent permission path.

The lease must outlast renewal and expire well before the stopping budget runs out.

These constraints dictate the lease rule for the machine. For continuous, uninterrupted trajectory tracking, the lease duration must exceed the proposer’s renewal interval plus an allowance for tail jitter. Simultaneously, to guarantee physical safety, the lease must fit within the pre-brake ceiling of equation 2 once detection latency, bus transport, actuator brake onset, and sensor evidence age at admission are deducted: \[\ChunkRenewalPeriod + \RenewalJitterAllowance < \ChunkLease \le \frac{\ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin - \SpeedAtDetection^2/2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{\SpeedAtDetection} - \PermissionLoopPeriod - \CommandTransportTime - \ActuatorResponseTime - \ObservationAge \tag{4}\] where \(\ChunkRenewalPeriod\) is the chunk policy’s nominal renewal period, \(\RenewalJitterAllowance\) is the margin allocated for interarrival timing variations, and \(\ObservationAge\) is charged as the evidence’s age at admission, rows 1 and 2 of table 1 together, not its age at dispatch alone. This inequality captures the coupled cyber-physical budget in a single mathematical relation. The left side reflects compute supply, bounded by the neural proposer. The first term on the right reflects physical mechanics, governed by vehicle speed, workspace clearance, and braking deceleration. The remaining subtractive terms account for detection and communication delays, actuator brake onset, and the observation age accumulated across the perception pipeline. A faster vehicle, a less aggressive brake, or a tighter workspace lowers the upper bound; a slower or more jitter-prone neural network raises the lower bound. If the two bounds cross, no lease can preserve both uninterrupted proposal tracking and the stopping guarantee at that velocity; the permission path must interrupt tracking before authority expires.

On the mobile manipulator, the lower bound evaluates to the 55 ms renewal floor derived in notebook 4.1. The upper bound provides 236.3 ms prior to deducting observation age; subtracting the 51.6 ms capture-to-dispatch age allocated in row 1 of table 1 leaves 184.7 ms. Because the observation must also traverse the dispatch-to-admission pipeline before execution, charging that interval’s budget from table 1 increases total evidence age at admission to 114.9 ms, leaving an effective upper limit of 121.4 ms. This is the same subtraction as the notebook’s, taken in a different order. The notebook charged the lease first and left 176.3 ms under the ceiling for evidence age and the stop profile; the lease rule charges the age first and leaves this limit for the lease. The selected 60 ms lease sits just above the lower renewal bound, reflecting the physical reality that every additional millisecond of lease duration consumes 1.5 mm of stopping clearance at maximum drive velocity. This value remains an unverified allocation until the physical vehicle demonstrates that all constituent intervals fit within budget under load. Artificially inflating the lease duration to mask software latency spikes merely consumes stopping clearance, trading mechanical safety margin for compute slack.

Because each chunk packages base velocities and arm joint targets together, the architecture enforces a single shared chunk lease across both subsystems. When that lease expires, each physical body must execute a kinematically feasible, state-matched fallback maneuver. The base’s 258.3 ms pre-brake ceiling governs this allocation; it does not imply that the arm possesses surplus margin. The arm analysis in The Planning Handoff and the Trajectory Record evaluates stopping distance and response travel against its own localized workspace clearances. Validating the shared lease requires verifying timing, evidence age, dynamic state, and fallback limits simultaneously across both base and manipulator. The goal the intent model supplies is governed by a different timer, the intent lease, which limits how long a high-level goal remains active before it must be renewed and is sized in its own chapter, not from this stopping budget.

↪ The intent lease and its contract are developed in The Intent Lease Specification and Contract.

Checkpoint 4.3: The handoffs matrix and physical budgets

Before testing the lease on a humanoid and reviewing the engineering fallacies, verify your understanding of system-wide interface contracts:

Systems Perspective 4.2: A deadline set by a fall

Question: Does the split among proposer, lease, and permission path still hold when the deadline comes from a fall rather than a stop?

An upright biped diverges from balance on the characteristic timescale \(\BalanceDivergenceTime = \sqrt{\MassCenterHeight/\Gravity}\), where \(\MassCenterHeight\) is its center-of-mass height. The biped is modeled in the sagittal plane as an inverted pendulum of point mass \(\Mass\) pivoting above a contact point at height \(\MassCenterHeight\) (the linear inverted pendulum model that Kajita et al. (2001) introduced for walking-pattern generation). Newton’s second law for a small angular deflection \(\TiltAngle\) from vertical then yields: \[\Mass \MassCenterHeight^2 \BipedTiltAcceleration = \Mass \Gravity \MassCenterHeight \sin\TiltAngle \implies \BipedTiltAcceleration \approx \frac{\Gravity}{\MassCenterHeight} \TiltAngle\] The characteristic equation \(\BipedTiltAcceleration - \PendulumNaturalFrequency^2 \TiltAngle = 0\) yields natural divergence modes \(e^{\pm \PendulumNaturalFrequency \TimeInstant}\) with natural frequency \(\PendulumNaturalFrequency = \sqrt{\Gravity/\MassCenterHeight}\). The growing exponential mode governs how quickly an unrecovered perturbation tilts out of control, with characteristic divergence timescale: \[\BalanceDivergenceTime = \frac{1}{\PendulumNaturalFrequency} = \sqrt{\frac{\MassCenterHeight}{\Gravity}}\] For an adult-scale humanoid with its center of mass near hip height (\(\MassCenterHeight =\) 0.9 m) and gravitational acceleration \(\Gravity = 9.81\,\text{m/s}^2\), this evaluates to \(\BalanceDivergenceTime = \sqrt{0.9 / 9.81} \approx 0.303\,\text{s} = 303\,\text{ms}\) (about 303 ms). This figure represents a characteristic divergence rate, not an absolute recovery deadline. The actual time available to recover depends on the biped’s initial angular deflection, its angular velocity, support polygon geometry, and available kinematic reach. Nonetheless, this timescale demonstrates why deliberative inference cannot be placed in the feedback loop governing local balance: an explicit, state-dependent recovery calculation must define the hard real-time deadline.

The architectural partition between proposer, lease, and permission path remains valid for bipeds, but the recovery deadline becomes dynamic and state-dependent. Rather than bringing drives to a standstill, balance recovery requires placing a foot at the dynamic capture point (Pratt et al. 2006), a process demanding both finite swing time and peak joint torque. The interface lease must accommodate these physical demands:

  • The lease is sized from the dynamic capture-point deadline. On a wheeled base, an expired lease triggers mechanical braking to bring the vehicle to rest (\(\SpeedAtDetection \cdot \ChunkLease\)). On an underactuated biped, locking the joints mid-stride makes the fall worse, because engaging holding brakes while the center of mass moves forward causes the torso to pivot forward into an uncontrolled ground impact. Where reach, swing-time, torque, and energy limits permit recovery, the lease fallback action must therefore execute an emergency capture-point step. The chunk lease, permission tick, fieldbus transit, and observation age must all fit within the dynamic recovery budget while reserving sufficient time to swing and plant the foot. The characteristic fall timescale frames this constraint but cannot substitute for dynamic simulation.
  • The proposer must meet the recovery budget. An inference renewal period that consumes a major fraction of the characteristic fall timescale cannot safely participate in posture stabilization. High-level intent models must remain decoupled from balance reflexes. Whether a mid-level chunk policy can safely control locomotion depends on whether its renewal interval and tail jitter leave adequate margin for fallback step execution.
  • Actuator thermal limits and supply sag constrain the recovery step. Executing an emergency recovery step demands simultaneous peak torque across multiple leg joints. This surge draws a large transient current, inducing resistive heating in motor windings and risking DC bus voltage collapse (Power distribution impedance, transient droop, and brownout resets). Consequently, the permission path must evaluate candidate recovery trajectories against instantaneous thermal and electrical reserves before admission.
Pratt, Jerry, John Carff, Sergey Drakunov, and Ambarish Goswami. 2006. “Capture Point: A Step Toward Humanoid Push Recovery.” Proceedings of the 6th IEEE-RAS International Conference on Humanoid Robots (Humanoids), 200–207. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICHR.2006.321385.
Kajita, Shuuji, Fumio Kanehiro, Kenji Kaneko, Kazuhito Yokoi, and Hirohisa Hirukawa. 2001. “The 3D Linear Inverted Pendulum Mode: A Simple Modeling for a Biped Walking Pattern Generation.” Proceedings of the 2001 IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 1: 239–46. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.2001.973365.

Whether the deadline comes from a warehouse rack end or a biped’s fall, the handoff matrix and the lease rule only protect the machine if the hardware and firmware underneath them honor the boundaries the equations assume. In practice, subtle implementation shortcuts in watchdog servicing, calibration ports, state feedback, and middleware configurations routinely bypass those boundaries without triggering a compile or bench error.

4.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A block diagram draws a clean boundary between high-level deliberation and low-level real-time enforcement, but physical boards and firmware routinely couple the two through shared power rails, common clock domains, interrupt lines, or auxiliary diagnostic ports. When a planning subsystem crashes or misbehaves, those hidden couplings decide whether the safety enforcement loop actually survives.

Pitfall: Servicing a safety watchdog inside a high-priority timer interrupt rather than an application-verified safety loop.

The mobile manipulator relies on a hardware self-watchdog to trigger the Safe Torque Off path of section 4.6 if it receives no feed within 5 ms. Because an enforcer that stops executing can no longer command a coordinated stop, this immediate hardware cutoff is required. A dangerous design mistake occurs when engineers service this watchdog from a high-priority hardware timer interrupt rather than directly from the safety loop. If the lower-priority enforcement task deadlocks on a mutex or crashes, the hardware timer interrupt continues firing every 1 ms tick. The watchdog remains fed while the enforcer has silently lost its ability to evaluate torque limits, kinematic clearances, and lease budgets.

In distributed systems engineering, this error is known as the confusion of liveness with correctness. A node responding to network ping heartbeats can be livelocked, thrashing on memory allocation, or deadlocked on an internal mutex. Servicing a watchdog must never signify mere scheduler activity. Instead, every refresh pulse must be conditioned on the successful, sequential retirement of all required verification stages, for example by requiring a monotonic task-completion token before a windowed hardware watchdog is serviced. An independent timer tick confirms only that interrupt dispatching still operates; it provides zero evidence that the controller has safely evaluated the machine’s physical state (section 4.4.1).

Architect’s countermeasure: Bind watchdog refresh pulses strictly to the verified completion of the safety loop’s invariant checks, deploying windowed hardware watchdogs that trip on both execution underruns and spurious early feeds.

Pitfall: Leaving auxiliary diagnostic or calibration interfaces unmediated in production operation.

Suppose the mobile manipulator validates base velocity proposals through its permission path, yet its drive-wheel motor drives retain an unmonitored serial calibration interface. If a diagnostic daemon writes a \(35.0\text{ A}\) test value directly into an inverter register through that port, the proposal mailbox continues reporting zero velocity. Checking the mailbox reveals nothing about the bypassed command, yet the drive wheels produce torque that no motion safety check ever evaluated or approved. Actuation authority is not defined by a logical software diagram; it encompasses every physical wire, bus transceiver, and digital interface capable of changing a power-stage register. Service interfaces must therefore incorporate operational interlocks or hardware jumpers that prevent calibration writes from bypassing the enforcer during production runs.

Architect’s countermeasure: Electrically isolate, physically jumper, or cryptographically interlock all auxiliary diagnostic and calibration ports during operational modes, routing all register writes exclusively through the real-time enforcer.

Fallacy: Evaluating safety barrier invariants on commanded trajectory setpoints is equivalent to evaluating physical plant state.

Suppose an arm controller evaluates safety using a control barrier function (Ames et al. 2019), a scalar function \(\BarrierFunction\) of the state space that remains positive within the safe set and is kept from going negative by filtering unacceptable control inputs. To conserve fieldbus bandwidth, an engineer might evaluate \(\BarrierFunction\) using commanded joint positions rather than load-side encoder feedback. If a transmission gear strips or a drive belt shears, the trajectory interpolator continues generating valid, smooth setpoints. The enforcer calculates that the system remains safely inside the barrier set (\(\BarrierFunction(\CommandedJointConfiguration) > 0\)), even while the decoupled arm collapses into an obstacle.
