Safety enforcement belongs at the causal boundary, which means it must ingest ground-truth physical state measurements through dependable sensory channels and account for sensor noise and latency. Auditing the command stream reveals what the planner intended, but only independent physical feedback captures where the machinery actually moves.

Ames, Aaron D., Samuel Coogan, Magnus Egerstedt, Gennaro Notomista, Koushil Sreenath, and Paulo Tabuada. 2019. “Control Barrier Functions: Theory and Applications.” 2019 18th European Control Conference (ECC), 3420–31. https://doi.org/10.23919/ECC.2019.8796030.

Architect’s countermeasure: Close safety barrier evaluations strictly around verified, independent sensor measurements (such as optical load encoders and current shunts) rather than planned trajectory setpoints.

↪ The control barrier function and the safety filter that keeps it nonnegative are developed in Safe Sets as Conditional Permission.

Fallacy: Nominal, fault-free operating logs are sufficient to prove physical architectural isolation.

Logging thousands of operating hours without a missed deadline demonstrates nominal stability, but clean telemetry cannot expose hidden cross-subsystem couplings that awaken only during failure modes. Subsystem isolation often breaks down under unmodeled physical interactions, such as supply-rail voltage sags when multiple inverters switch simultaneously, bus contention storms, or memory corruption spilling across addressing domains. Rigorous qualification requires deliberate fault injection: inducing power transients, flooding communication buses, corrupting policy memory, and mechanically locking motor shafts on a dynamometer. Each experiment must evaluate an explicit, quantitative response bound, such as locked-rotor detection clamping motor current within \(2.0\text{ ms}\), rather than treating the mere absence of an operating system crash as validation. Fault experiments support the isolation claim within their stated fault and load coverage when the system maintains invariant enforcement or reaches the specified fallback within the quantitative response bounds. They do not establish coverage of untested fault combinations.

Architect’s countermeasure: Validate fault-domain independence through active fault-injection regimes (power sags, clock glitches, bus flooding, and memory corruption) with quantitative pass-fail response bounds.

Pitfall: Assuming zero packet loss in robotics middleware equates to deterministic real-time delivery.

A publish-subscribe transport configured for reliable delivery attempts to recover lost packets, but successful recovery does not establish deterministic real-time delivery. After congestion or interference, a reliable transport delivers the burst of queued, obsolete setpoints that section 4.3.2 traced, each describing a robot state that no longer exists. While switching to a freshest-sample policy prevents queue backlogs, dropping stale frames does not inherently bound scheduling jitter, serialization delay, or driver execution latency. The cycle-critical actuation path requires two separate guarantees: an engineered worst-case delivery bound across the physical communication bus and an explicit freshness deadline enforced by the receiver before executing any torque command.

Architect’s countermeasure: Budget the complete transport response under declared traffic and fault conditions, enforce deadline-based freshness at the consumer, and isolate cycle-critical commands from supervisory middleware traffic regardless of the bus architecture.

4.11 Summary

The Physical Body derived the physical stopping budget for the loaded machine, and The Cognitive Brain established the operational cadences for proposal generation and evidence age. This chapter introduced the real-time permission path designed to enforce those constraints, establishing a two-processor architecture whose physical isolation still awaits evaluation under load. To guarantee deterministic safety, the permission path holds exclusive write access to the motor drive registers. It communicates with the unprivileged proposal engine through a lock-free mailbox that never stalls the high-frequency control tick, while communication and computation delays mandate closing joint-level feedback loops directly within the drives. By evaluating command interfaces, from joint positions to operational-space impedances, the permission path prices each check against available stopping clearance. Combining these constraints yields a bounded time window during which an action proposal is permitted to drive physical actuators. On the running machine, this analysis produces a chunk lease that exceeds the proposer’s renewal interval while fitting within the pre-brake delay budget. The resulting handoff record documents this lease alongside its validity conditions and evidence requirements. This lease and the mechanisms behind it remain claims until they are verified under worst-case compute jitter, bus contention, malformed proposals, sensor faults, and degraded braking. Table 5 summarizes the four governing relations that define the nervous system’s timing, lease duration, and response guarantees, along with the engineering decisions they resolve.

Key Takeaways: The permission path and determinism
  • The permission path alone writes the drives: Learned models act only as unprivileged proposal engines (\(\CandidateProposal_t\)). Write access to the motor drive registers belongs to the deterministic real-time enforcer alone, hosted on silicon whose isolation from the proposer must be verified experimentally.
  • Cadence is dictated by physics, not software: The four-tier concurrency hierarchy (20 kHz FOC current regulation, 1 kHz kinematic enforcer checks, 20 Hz action-chunk renewal, and 2 Hz multimodal deliberation on the running machine) is governed by physical time constants (\(\ElectricalTimeConstant = \PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\)), total loop delay, exposure horizons, and model execution budgets. The total loop delay appears as a phase lag \(\DelayPhaseLag = \CrossoverFrequency(\SamplingPeriod/2 + \EnforcerComputeTime + \CommandTransportTime)\), which keeps the joint loops in the drives.
  • The exchange never waits, and every proposal expires: The permission path reads the mailbox in a single bounded attempt per tick, retaining the currently active, independently admissible chunk if a write collision occurs. Every proposal carries an evidence timestamp and an explicit expiration horizon that terminates its authority even if an explicit cancellation never arrives.
  • Stopping dynamics dimension the chunk lease: For the mobile manipulator operating at its drive limits, the pre-brake ceiling is 258.3 ms. Sizing the lease at 60 ms places it just above the proposer’s renewal floor. The interval from the final admitted chunk to brake onset takes 82 ms. Its lease path brings the stopping budget’s running total to 835.5 mm of the 1.10 m clear distance before evidence age and the remaining stop margins are charged (row 4 of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget).
  • Worst-case bounds outrank empirical distributions: Tail-latency percentiles such as \(\text{p99}\) cannot establish real-time safety. Deterministic enforcement requires bounding worst-case computation, blocking, and interference (\(\WorstCaseResponseTime_i = \WorstCaseExecutionTime_i + \TaskBlockingTime_i + \TaskInterferenceTime_i \le \TaskRelativeDeadline_i\)) under rigorously qualified operating conditions.
  • The permission path is only as independent as what it shares: If the permission path shares a clock source, power rail, memory bus, or silicon die with the proposal engine, faults in the proposer can compromise the enforcer. The two-processor split is an architectural hypothesis that requires empirical validation through stress testing and fault injection (Empirical Verification of Silicon Isolation), not an assumed property of separate software processes.
  • The command interface sets what the permission path must check: Joint position targets express kinematic intent that the enforcer can verify geometrically, but they cannot bound contact forces. Velocity interfaces require a bounded motion projection, while torque interfaces require dynamic bounds and a feasible fallback. Impedance targets bound steady-state interaction forces, but only if the enforcer validates the stiffness and damping gains alongside the reference trajectory.
  • Locate interface regressions at their handoff: The system handoff record maps every timing and clearance limit to one of five architectural boundaries, binding each to operational preconditions, evidence categories, and bound types. When performance regresses, engineers can isolate the offending subsystem against its allocated budget using like-for-like evidence.

Physical stopping limits, inference renewal rates, and the Nervous System’s response delays converge on one governing parameter, how long an admitted proposal may command the actuators. When a timing question arises on a new machine, the first diagnostic is to price that parameter, converting the lease path to distance at the operating speed and comparing it with what the stopping budget has left. A lease that cannot fit between the renewal floor and the pre-brake ceiling cannot sustain uninterrupted proposal tracking at that speed, and a faster processor helps only if it lets the renewal period, and with it the floor, shrink.

This chapter closes Part I, and with it the argument that the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), set in motion. The Body turned irreversibility into distances and limit records, the Brain into proposals that arrive late and expire, and the Nervous System into a rule of authority. Motion that cannot be undone may be granted only for as long as the stopping budget can still guarantee the stop. The chunk lease is that rule written as a number, and the handoff record is where the number is kept. Everything the machine later learns or is permitted to do inherits this bound, including the demonstrations it collects while the permission path is watching.

4.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The phase-margin arguments that keep high-bandwidth joint feedback loops inside local motor drives predate digital computing. Nyquist (1932) established the foundational stability criterion that determines whether a closed-loop feedback amplifier will oscillate, and Bode (1945) formalized these dynamics into the gain and phase margins that control engineers budget today. When mechanical and analog controllers transitioned to software, sampled-data control theory formalized how digital sample-and-hold stages, discrete computation steps, and communication transport introduce pure time delays that consume this margin (Franklin et al. 1998). High-bandwidth feedback-loop placement in this chapter follows directly from this phase accounting: a control loop must close physically close to its sensors and actuators because any transport latency erodes phase margin that high-bandwidth physical dynamics cannot spare.

Joseph, Mathai, and Paritosh Pandya. 1986. “Finding Response Times in a Real-Time System.” The Computer Journal 29 (5): 390–95. https://doi.org/10.1093/comjnl/29.5.390.
Audsley, Neil, Alan Burns, Mike Richardson, Ken Tindell, and Andy J. Wellings. 1993. “Applying New Scheduling Theory to Static Priority Pre-Emptive Scheduling.” Software Engineering Journal 8 (5): 284–92. https://doi.org/10.1049/sej.1993.0034.
Reeves, Glenn E. 1997. What Really Happened on Mars? Electronic mail message, Jet Propulsion Laboratory.

Predictable execution and real-time scheduling supply the second pillar of this architecture. Liu and Layland (1973) proved that assigning static priorities monotonically by rate guarantees schedulability for periodic task sets up to a theoretical utilization bound. Subsequent response-time analysis transformed this sufficient condition into an exact test by summing worst-case execution time, resource blocking, and higher-priority task interference (Joseph and Pandya 1986; Audsley et al. 1993). When shared resources threatened these guarantees with priority inversion, Sha et al. (1990) bounded the duration of blocking through priority inheritance and the priority ceiling protocol. The Mars Pathfinder mission in 1997 demonstrated precisely what the absence of such bounds costs in production, when unmanaged priority inversions triggered repeated system resets on the Martian surface (Reeves 1997). Extending predictability to distributed embedded systems, Kopetz (2011) established the foundations of time-triggered design. That work argued that safety-critical systems must remain statically analyzable prior to execution, and it introduced the concepts of state messages and temporal accuracy that govern the proposal headers in this chapter.

The lock-free coordination between the high-level policy proposer and the low-level safety permission path descends from foundational work on sharing state without waiting. Lamport (1977) demonstrated that a concurrent reader can detect an overlapping write using version sequence numbers alone. Simpson (1990) developed the asynchronous four-slot mechanism for flight-control avionics running on unrelated clocks, Herlihy (1991) formalized the theoretical hierarchy of wait-free synchronization, and Kopetz and Reisinger (1993) formulated the non-blocking write protocol specifically for real-time readers. Industrial networks confronted similar timing and isolation challenges on the wire: Controller Area Network (CAN) bus architectures resolved medium access through deterministic, bitwise priority arbitration (International Organization for Standardization 2015). The functional-safety profiles of IEC 61784-3 established the black-channel principle, which encapsulates integrity and freshness guarantees within the messages so that the transmission medium itself need not be trusted (International Electrotechnical Commission 2021). Finally, the concept of bounded temporal leases originated in distributed systems, where Gray and Cheriton (1989) restricted how long a client could rely on cached data so that an unresponsive client blocked the server for at most one lease period. Physical AI systems adapt this lease mechanism to an entirely different physical objective: rather than preserving distributed cache consistency, the lease is bounded by the kinetic stopping budget and renewal deadlines of the safety monitor.

Kopetz, Hermann, and Johannes Reisinger. 1993. “The Non-Blocking Write Protocol NBW: A Solution to a Real-Time Synchronization Problem.” Proceedings of the 1993 Real-Time Systems Symposium, 131–37. https://doi.org/10.1109/REAL.1993.393507.
International Organization for Standardization. 2015. ISO 11898-1:2015: Road Vehicles, Controller Area Network (CAN), Part 1: Data Link Layer and Physical Signalling. International Organization for Standardization.
International Electrotechnical Commission. 2021. IEC 61784-3:2021: Industrial Communication Networks, Profiles, Part 3: Functional Safety Fieldbuses, General Rules and Profile Definitions. International Electrotechnical Commission.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.

For readers seeking deeper exploration, six works trace the lineage from classical feedback theory to modern real-time permission paths:

  • Franklin et al. (1998) provides the definitive treatment of digital and sampled-data control, analyzing how zero-order hold circuits, computation time, and transport latencies introduce phase lag. This analysis forms the theoretical foundation for the multi-rate cadence decisions in section 4.3.
  • Liu and Layland (1973) is a concise, classic paper that remains the clearest demonstration of how rate-monotonic priority assignment guarantees schedulability under worst-case task arrival patterns.
  • Sha et al. (1990) formalizes the priority inheritance and priority ceiling protocols alongside their mathematical blocking bounds. This work explains both the recovery strategy deployed on Mars Pathfinder and the derivation of the blocking term \(\TaskBlockingTime_i\) in equation 3.
  • Buttazzo (2011) offers an authoritative textbook treatment of hard real-time systems, fixed-priority and deadline-driven scheduling, and response-time analysis, complete with concrete task sets that readers can verify by hand.
  • Kopetz (2011) develops real-time architectures from the time-triggered perspective. Its chapters on real-time entities, temporal accuracy, and communication clarify why our proposal mailbox exchanges idempotent state messages rather than transient event queues.
  • Simpson (1990) presents the four-slot mechanism in its original flight-critical avionics context, demonstrating how four memory slots decouple an asynchronous reader and writer without locking, waiting, or sample loss.
Franklin, Gene F, J David Powell, and Michael L Workman. 1998. Digital Control of Dynamic Systems. 3rd ed. Addison-Wesley.
Sha, Lui, Ragunathan Rajkumar, and John P. Lehoczky. 1990. “Priority Inheritance Protocols: An Approach to Real-Time Synchronization.” IEEE Transactions on Computers 39 (9): 1175–85.
Buttazzo, Giorgio C. 2011. Hard Real-Time Computing Systems: Predictable Scheduling Algorithms and Applications. 3rd ed. Real-Time Systems Series. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-0676-1.
Kopetz, Hermann. 2011. Real-Time Systems: Design Principles for Distributed Embedded Applications. 2nd ed. Real-Time Systems Series. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4419-8237-7.
Simpson, H. R. 1990. “Four-Slot Fully Asynchronous Communication Mechanism.” IEE Proceedings E (Computers and Digital Techniques) 137 (1): 17–30. https://doi.org/10.1049/ip-e.1990.0002.

What’s Next: From anatomy to closed-loop learning
When an embodied machine modifies or rejects a human command before execution, the recorded demonstration no longer reflects unconstrained intent. The Body, the Brain, and the Nervous System establish physical limits, learned proposals, and a timed permission path. During data collection, such as teleoperated openings of the spring-latched cage door, that path may clamp, rate-limit, or refuse an operator’s request, while the body may deliver motion that differs from the admitted command. The limit and handoff records preserve the conditions behind those interventions. In Part II, Physical Data begins from the reality that a physical machine manufactures its own training data, so each executed action changes the observations that follow. Each record carries what the operator requested, what the permission path admitted, and what the body delivered. It then establishes what a demonstration must retain so that a policy can learn the task without mistaking the collecting machine’s limitations for intended behavior.
